720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"security-impact\"\u003ESecurity Impact\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBoth vulnerabilities are unauthenticated and exploitable over the local network. Devices exposed to the internet through port forwarding or UPnP are especially at risk. Successful exploitation provides root-level access to the camera with no user interaction.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBecause the exploit path bypasses firmware integrity checks, attackers can load unsigned payloads or persist via custom daemons, making cleanup difficult.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"recommendations\"\u003E&nbsp;Recommendations\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EUsers should avoid exposing the Dahua camera web interface of vulnerable models to the internet.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;Disable UPnP and remove port forwarding rules if present.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EIsolate the camera on a separate VLAN\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;or dedicated IoT network to limit lateral movement.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EMonitor vendor updates\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;and apply patches as soon as available. As of writing, firmware versions after 2025\u002F04\u002F16 fix the issues.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E",tags:[{id:e,name:f,slug:c,description:a,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a},{id:Y,name:A,slug:R,description:a,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a},{id:O,name:P,slug:Q,description:a,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a}],authors:[{id:J,name:K,slug:L,profile_image:M,cover_image:a,bio:N,website:a,location:a,facebook:a,twitter:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a}],excerpt:"Researchers at Bitdefender have identified critical security vulnerabilities in the firmware of the Dahua Hero C1 (DH-H4C) smart camera series. The flaws, affecting the device's ONVIF protocol and file upload handlers, allow unauthenticated attackers to execute arbitrary commands remotely, effectively taking over the device.\n\nThe vulnerabilities were reported to Dahua for responsible mitigation and disclosure and are now patched at the time of publication.\n\n\nAffected Devices\n\nThe issues were ver",reading_time:bj,url:"\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Fvulnerabilities-identified-in-dahua-hero-c1-smart-cameras\u002F"},{id:"66d5c3d2d0ec1aefc7d722d2",title:"60 Hurts per Second – How We Got Access to Enough Solar Power to Run the United States",slug:"60-hurts-per-second-how-we-got-access-to-enough-solar-power-to-run-the-united-states",feature_image:"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2024\u002F08\u002F1ZZ96uESRJQ.jpg",featured:d,published_at:"2024-08-07T16:16:00.000+03:00",custom_excerpt:a,html:"\u003Cp\u003EThe electricity grid – the buzzing, crackling marvel that supplies the lifeblood of modernity - is by far the largest structure humanity ever built. It’s so big, in fact, that few people even notice it, like a fish can’t see the ocean.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUntil the grid goes down, that is. Then, like the fish dangling from the angler’s hook, we see our vulnerability. Modernity dissolves into a sudden silence, followed by the repeated flick of a light switch, and a howl of panic at the prospect of missed appointments, ruined dinners, lost workdays, stopped elevators and dark, cold evenings, and worse.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBut most of the time, the grid hums along just fine ...\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENow, take that labyrinthine system and add another complex network, this one delivering hundreds of gigawatts of power from the sun - solar power infrastructure. This ever-fluctuating source of energy is tapped via rooftop panels, distant solar farms, slapdash home arrays and other evolving infrastructure around the globe.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnd with complexity comes risk ...\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThen, take those two systems and intersect them with the Internet of Things, an ethereal network that runs from the limits of human thought – both positive and malicious – into the routines of daily life through seemingly innocuous gadgets.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt that intersection we find the mundane world of inverters and controllers.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnd that's where a group of researchers stumbled upon an alarming fact:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003Ea solar grid responsible for 195 gigawatts, or 20 percent of the world’s solar power output, is vulnerable. That’s enough to power the entire United States - and it’s just waiting to be hijacked.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-findings\"\u003EKey Findings\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EBitdefender researchers have identified a series of vulnerabilities in PV plant management platforms operated by Solarman and Deye.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EThis platform is responsible for coordinating production operations of millions of solar installations worldwide generating a whopping output of approximately 195 GW of solar power (20% of the global solar production)\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EIf exploited, these vulnerabilities could allow an attacker to control inverter settings that could take parts of the grid down, potentially causing blackouts.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EThese vulnerabilities have been communicated to the affected vendors and fixed.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"understanding-the-power-grid\"\u003EUnderstanding the Power Grid\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe power grid is a vast network of power plants, transmission lines and distribution systems that delivers electricity from producers to consumers. It consists of three main components:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Col\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EGeneration\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: Power plants generate electricity using various sources, including coal, natural gas, nuclear, hydro and, increasingly, solar and wind.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ETransmission\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: High-voltage transmission lines carry electricity over long distances from power plants to substations.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EDistribution\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: Substations lower the voltage for safe distribution to homes and businesses through local power lines.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Fol\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe grid balances supply and demand in real time, ensuring that electricity is available when needed. When generation exceeds demand, voltage in the grid rises to dangerous levels that can damage sensitive equipment connected to it.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhen generation can't meet demand, anomalies begin to occur. Initially, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Farstechnica.com\u002Ftech-policy\u002F2018\u002F03\u002Fovens-across-europe-display-the-wrong-time-due-to-a-serbia-kosovo-grid-dispute\u002F\"\u003Eyour microwave oven may get several minutes slower\u003C\u002Fa\u003E (as it computes its current time based on the “ticks” in the network measured in Hertz: 60 Hertz - or cycles per second - in the US, and 50 Hz in Europe). Secondly, parts of the grid where the imbalance is detected are disconnected before they drag the whole system into a downwards spiral that can take days to recover from.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2024\u002F08\u002Fimage.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt loading=\"lazy\" width=\"904\" height=\"500\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2024\u002F08\u002Fimage.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2024\u002F08\u002Fimage.png 904w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFascinated? To learn more about how the grid works, you might want to watch \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.youtube.com\u002Fwatch?v=v1BMWczn7JM\"\u003Ethis episode of Practical Engineering\u003C\u002Fa\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"the-role-of-solar-energy-in-the-grid\"\u003EThe Role of Solar Energy in the Grid\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESolar energy has a powerful impact on the energy landscape. Solar panels convert sunlight into a clean, renewable source of power, which can be used immediately, stored in batteries, or fed into the grid.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESolar power systems are often decentralized, meaning they are installed on individual homes or businesses rather than centralized power plants. This means that generation and distribution often take place in the same ZIP code and (with some exceptions such as solar power production in parks), disruptions at this level have a limited geographic impact.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis decentralization presents several challenges and opportunities for the grid. Solar power reduces dependence on fossil fuels, lowers greenhouse gas emissions, and can enhance grid resilience by distributing power generation. However, integrating numerous small-scale solar systems into the grid can complicate management.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESolar setups produce variable amounts of electricity, and only during daytime.  Maintaining voltage levels and managing the variability of solar power must  be outsourced to a pre-programmed critical component called an inverter. An inverter converts the direct current (DC) electricity generated by solar panels into alternating current (AC) electricity, the standard used by most homes and businesses, as well as for transmission over the power grid.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe inverter performs two primary functions in a solar power system:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EDC to AC Conversion\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: Solar panels generate DC electricity  however, most electrical appliances and the power grid operate on AC electricity, which alternates in polarity and voltage. The inverter converts the DC electricity into AC electricity to  be used by household appliances or fed into the grid.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EGrid Synchronization\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: In grid-tied solar power systems, the inverter  synchronizes the phase and frequency of the AC output with the grid. This ensures that the electricity generated by the solar panels is compatible with the grid and can be safely exported to it. Differences in phase and voltage can crash the grid.  Power distributors and governments  see any deliberate attempts to bypass these grid safety measures as a threat to national security.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"security-flaws-in-solar-management-platforms\"\u003ESecurity Flaws in Solar Management Platforms\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender researchers  started looking into potential cybercriminal use of  inverters, as they are becoming increasingly visible on the radar of internet-connected devices. By the foresight of “we had one already available and we wanted to test it,” we  looked into data loggers made by Solarman that are a popular choice for monitoring Deye inverter units..\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo add context, Solarman is one of the world’s largest photovoltaic (PV) monitoring and management platform, and equipment connected to this platform is apparently responsible for the production of over \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.solarmanpv.com\u002Fcorporate\u002Fabout-us\u002F\"\u003E195 gigawatts of power across 2M+ active PV plants involving \"10M+ devices in 190+ countries and territories”.\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA quick look at the platform shows Solarman is not only an equipment builder,  they also license parts of their infrastructure to other manufacturers (see the Appendix). Our analysis shows that the Solarman API architecture has multiple entry points exposed for multiple companies selling inverters, data loggers and a wide range of PV equipment. The investigation of the Deye inverter and Solarman data logger have uncovered severe security vulnerabilities that could be exploited to disrupt or even take down the grid.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003ENote - a data logger in an inverter setup records and transmits data on the solar power system's performance. It enables real-time and historical monitoring, fault detection, and remote management, ensuring the system operates efficiently and reliably. By gathering metrics such as power output, voltage and current, the data logger helps optimize energy production and usage, facilitates maintenance through timely alerts, and integrates with cloud platforms for accessible remote monitoring. This management feature improves system performance, enhances reliability, and supports informed decision-making, ultimately contributing to the longevity and effectiveness of the solar power installation.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe Solarman platform is linked to a variety of equipment and brands revolving around it. Many of these customers seem to use the entire platform as a service, while others wrap API endpoints in custom implementations and keep customer databases private.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBeyond the multiple entry-points exposed by the platform (discussed above), we sought to discover how various parts of this architecture fit together. To validate our assumptions, we successfully attempted to register a Solarman data logger into the Deye platform.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAs far as we are able to tell, Deye has been using the original Solarman infrastructure up until 2024, but they \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.deyeinverter.com\u002Fnews\u002Fcompany-news\u002Fdeye-cloud-account-transfer.html\"\u003Ehave customized their implementation\u003C\u002Fa\u003E and spun off a new datacenter to accommodate their own user base.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe Solarman platform has two types of accounts: one is regular reporting, where the logged-in user has access to production and usage data in real time, while the other is a business (contractor) account used by the authorized installation company to control devices, modify voltage or frequency and control other power grid injection settings). With these details out of the way, it’s time to dig into how we managed to gain access to all business and regular accounts in all solar production devices made by Solarman and its partners.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"vulnerabilities-in-the-solarman-platform\"\u003EVulnerabilities in the Solarman platform\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOur research on the Solarman platform uncovered the following vulnerabilities: (These flaws are detailed in a separate paper available below.)\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-card kg-file-card kg-file-card-medium\"\u003E\n            \u003Ca class=\"kg-file-card-container\" href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Ffiles\u002F2024\u002F08\u002FBitdefender-PReport-solarman-creat7907.pdf\" title=\"Download\" download\u003E\n                \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-contents\"\u003E\n                    \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-title\"\u003EVulnerabilities in the Solarman platform\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                    \n                    \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-metadata\"\u003E\n                        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-filename\"\u003EBitdefender-PReport-solarman-creat7907.pdf\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-filesize\"\u003E974 KB\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                    \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-icon\"\u003E\n                    \u003Csvg xmlns=\"http:\u002F\u002Fwww.w3.org\u002F2000\u002Fsvg\" viewBox=\"0 0 24 24\"\u003E\u003Cdefs\u003E\u003Cstyle\u003E.a{fill:none;stroke:currentColor;stroke-linecap:round;stroke-linejoin:round;stroke-width:1.5px;}\u003C\u002Fstyle\u003E\u003C\u002Fdefs\u003E\u003Ctitle\u003Edownload-circle\u003C\u002Ftitle\u003E\u003Cpolyline class=\"a\" points=\"8.25 14.25 12 18 15.75 14.25\"\u002F\u003E\u003Cline class=\"a\" x1=\"12\" y1=\"6.75\" x2=\"12\" y2=\"18\"\u002F\u003E\u003Ccircle class=\"a\" cx=\"12\" cy=\"12\" r=\"11.25\"\u002F\u003E\u003C\u002Fsvg\u003E\n                \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n            \u003C\u002Fa\u003E\n        \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n        \u003Col\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EFull Account Takeover\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: The Solarman platform’s \u003Ccode\u003E\u002Foauth2-s\u002Foauth\u002Ftoken\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E API endpoint lets attackers generate authorization tokens for any account. By modifying the JSON payload, attackers can gain control over any regular or business account. This means that a malicious user could iterate through all accounts, take over any of them and modify inverter parameters or change the way the inverter interacts with the grid.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EToken Reuse Across Platforms\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: JWT tokens issued by the Deye Cloud platform are also valid on the Solarman platform, granting full access to the accounts based on their ID. This cross-platform vulnerability can lead to widespread unauthorized access in the absence of well-defined boundaries to isolate vendors.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EExcessive Data Exposure\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: The Solarman platform’s API endpoints return excessive information about organizations, including private details such as email addresses and phone numbers. This information can be harvested to exploit the platform’s users and organizations. In addition to personally identifiable information, an attacker can chain this with the first vulnerability to run subsequent queries on the account and obtain GPS coordinates for solar installations as well as their real-time production capacity.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Fol\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"vulnerabilities-in-the-deye-platform\"\u003EVulnerabilities in the Deye platform\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Col\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EHard-Coded Credentials\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: The Deye platform uses a hard-coded account (`SmartConfigurator@solarmanpv.com` with password \u003Ccode\u003E123456\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E) to access device data. This account can obtain an authorization token that grants access to any device, exposing sensitive information such as software versions, Wi-Fi credentials, and more​ (see the Deye disclosure report available below)​.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EInformation Leakage\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: The API endpoint \u003Ccode\u003E\u002Fuser-s\u002Facc\u002Forgs\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E on eu1.deyecloud.com returns excessive private information about users, including names, email addresses, phone numbers and user IDs. This endpoint can be exploited to leak information about all users on the platform​.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EAuthorization Token Generation\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: The \u003Ccode\u003E\u002Foauth-s\u002Foauth\u002F\u003C\u002Fcode\u003Etoken API endpoint has the same vulnerability as the endpoint used by Solarman. However, the JWT token that is generated contains a wrong value and is not accepted by the server, even though the token is valid and signed. This issue could pose a future risk if not properly patched​.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Fol\u003E\n        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-card kg-file-card kg-file-card-medium\"\u003E\n            \u003Ca class=\"kg-file-card-container\" href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Ffiles\u002F2024\u002F08\u002FBitdefender-PReport-deye-creat7907.pdf\" title=\"Download\" download\u003E\n                \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-contents\"\u003E\n                    \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-title\"\u003EVulnerabilities in the Deye platform\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                    \n                    \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-metadata\"\u003E\n                        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-filename\"\u003EBitdefender-PReport-deye-creat7907.pdf\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-filesize\"\u003E2 MB\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                    \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-icon\"\u003E\n                    \u003Csvg xmlns=\"http:\u002F\u002Fwww.w3.org\u002F2000\u002Fsvg\" viewBox=\"0 0 24 24\"\u003E\u003Cdefs\u003E\u003Cstyle\u003E.a{fill:none;stroke:currentColor;stroke-linecap:round;stroke-linejoin:round;stroke-width:1.5px;}\u003C\u002Fstyle\u003E\u003C\u002Fdefs\u003E\u003Ctitle\u003Edownload-circle\u003C\u002Ftitle\u003E\u003Cpolyline class=\"a\" points=\"8.25 14.25 12 18 15.75 14.25\"\u002F\u003E\u003Cline class=\"a\" x1=\"12\" y1=\"6.75\" x2=\"12\" y2=\"18\"\u002F\u003E\u003Ccircle class=\"a\" cx=\"12\" cy=\"12\" r=\"11.25\"\u002F\u003E\u003C\u002Fsvg\u003E\n                \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n            \u003C\u002Fa\u003E\n        \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n        \u003Ch2 id=\"implications-for-grid-security\"\u003EImplications for Grid Security\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhile most of our previous findings have a serious impact on the individual or on the internet itself, the flaws detailed in this research are fundamentally different. Access to devices interacting with the grid can have devastating effects on the proper functioning of the grid itself. These vulnerabilities pose a significant threat to grid security in several ways:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EUnauthorized Control\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: Attackers can take over accounts and control solar inverters, disrupting power generation and potentially causing voltage fluctuations.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EData Breaches\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: Sensitive information about users and organizations can be leaked, leading to privacy violations, information harvesting, targeted phishing attacks or other malicious activities.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EOperational Disruptions\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: By accessing and modifying settings on solar inverters, attackers can cause widespread disruptions in power distribution, impacting grid stability and potentially leading to blackouts.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"disclosure-timeline\"\u003EDisclosure Timeline\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"solarman\"\u003ESolarman:\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMay 22, 2024: Bitdefender reaches out to Solarman for a security contact\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMay 23, 2024: Bitdefender gets in touch with Solarman security team and sends vulnerability information\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMay 24, 2024: Vulnerabilities acknowledged; account takeover gets immediately fixed\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJun 17, 2024: Vendor confirms that a fix for the API returning too much information is in place.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJul 17, 2024: Vendor confirms that the Deye token reuse issue is now fixed.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"deye\"\u003EDeye:\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMay 22, 2024: Bitdefender reaches out to Deye for a security contact\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJun 03, 2024: Authorized security partner asks Bitdefender for details\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJun 06, 2024: Bitdefender sends the vulnerability report\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJun 17, 2024: Bitdefender asks for an update on the reported issues\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJul 09, 2024: Deye sends an overview of the fixed issues\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EAug 07, 2024: This report becomes public as per the coordinated vulnerability disclosure protocols.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"conclusion\"\u003EConclusion\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIntegrating solar power into the grid offers immense benefits, but it also introduces attack surfaces that equipment makers must take into account. The security flaws found in the Deye and Solarman platforms highlight the need for robust cybersecurity in managing solar energy systems, as well as in general IoT setups.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EProtecting the grid from cyber threats is crucial to ensuring reliable and secure power for all. As we continue to embrace renewable energy, we must also remain vigilant and proactive in securing our energy infrastructure against evolving threats.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe’d like to thank the vendors involved for acknowledging and fixing these vulnerabilities in a timely manner.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EToo long for a read? Watch this piece of research as a video presentation on August 9 at 5\u003C\u002Fem\u003E:00PM (Creator Stage 2\u003Cem\u003E @ the IoT Village talks) at Defcon. If you are attending Defcon 32 in person, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.iotvillage.org\u002Fdefcon.html\"\u003Ecome see the talk live\u003C\u002Fa\u003E or meet and greet the researchers.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"appendix\"\u003EAppendix\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHere is a list of Solarman partner companies using the platform and potentially affected by the reported vulnerabilities. Our testing reveals that most of these vendors have scheduled maintentance windows to deploy security patches. We presume that these scheduled maintenance intervals are set to implement mitigations for the issues we reported to Solarman and Deye.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003C!--kg-card-begin: This report documents four vulnerabilities affecting devices powered by the ThroughTek Kalay Platform. Due to the platform’s massive presence in IoT integrations, these flaws have a significant downstream impact on several vendors.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn the interconnected landscape of the Internet of Things (IoT), the reliability and security of devices, infrastructure and data are paramount. Among the many frameworks facilitating the operation of IoT devices, ThroughTek's Kalay platform stands as a linchpin, powering \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.throughtek.com\u002Fadvantages\u002F\"\u003Emore than 100 million devices worldwide\u003C\u002Fa\u003E. With a predominant presence in surveillance cameras and security devices, ThroughTek Kalay's influence underscores its significance in safeguarding homes, businesses, and integrators alike.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003ENOTE: the vulnerabilities presented in this paper have been responsibly disclosed to the affected vendors. Specific firmware information is available in each report below. We would like to extend our thanks to the involved vendors for their prompt acknowledgement of vulnerability and rapid patch release.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"timeline\"\u003E\u003Cem\u003ETimeline\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EOct 19, 2023: Bitdefender contacts ThroghTek and sends the vulnerability report.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EOct 20, 2023: Vendor confirms the issues.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EOct 26, 2023: Vendor asks for over 90-day time extension to implement and deploy the fix.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMar 15, 2024: Additional extension is requested.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EApr 12, 2024: Coordinated vulnerability disclosure is scheduled for May 15 2024 to allow all affected parties to apply the patches\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EApr 16, 2024: Vendor confirms that all affected SDK versions have been patched.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMay 15, 2024: This report becomes public.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"vulnerabilities-exposed\"\u003EVulnerabilities Exposed\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6321\"\u003ECVE-2023-6321\u003C\u002Fa\u003E allows an authenticated user to run system commands as the root user leading to the full compromise of the device.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6322\"\u003ECVE-2023-6322\u003C\u002Fa\u003E  enables attackers to gain root access through a stack-based buffer overflow vulnerability in the handler of an IOCTL message, typically employed in configuring motion detection zones in cameras. This is a vulnerability specific to some devices that use motion detection features.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6323\"\u003ECVE-2023-6323\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, for instance, exposes a loophole wherein a local attacker can illicitly obtain the AuthKey secret, effectively helping an attacker establish a preliminary connection to the victim device.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003ELastly, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6324\"\u003ECVE-2023-6324 \u003C\u002Fa\u003Eexploits a vulnerability allowing attackers to infer the pre-shared key for a DTLS session, a critical prerequisite for connecting and talking to the victim devices.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhen chained together, these vulnerabilities facilitate unauthorized root access from within the local network, as well as remote* code execution to completely subvert the victim device.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E*Remote code execution is only possible after the device has been probed from the local network.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"affected-vendors\"\u003EAffected vendors\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhile these vulnerabilities affect the TUTK platform and subsequently impact most implementations, our research was conducted on three major devices sold worldwide. Given that some vendors had device-specific vulnerabilities, individual timelines are available in each report.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"owlet-cam-v1-and-v2\"\u003EOwlet Cam v1 and v2\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOwlet Cam uses the ThroughTek Kalay solution to communicate with clients over the Internet.  The three vulnerabilities (\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6323\"\u003ECVE-2023-6323\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6324\"\u003ECVE-2023-6324\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, and \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6321\"\u003ECVE-2023-6321\u003C\u002Fa\u003E) can be chained together to allow an attacker to obtain root access from the local network and then to execute commands on the device. On Owlet Cam, command execution is obtained via \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6321\"\u003ECVE-2023-6321\u003C\u002Fa\u003E - a vulnerability in IOCTL message 0x6008E, which is used to unpack archives containing OTA updates.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA technical dive into the vulnerabilities and how they are daisy-chained to compromise the Owlet Cam is available below:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003C!--kg-card-begin: html--\u003E\u003Cp align=\"center\"\u003E\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Ffiles\u002F2024\u002F05\u002FBitdefender-PReport-owlet-7745.pdf\" class=\"button-view-all d-inline-block\"\u003EDownload the whitepaper\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003C!--kg-card-end: html--\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"wyze-cam-v3\"\u003EWyze Cam v3\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender researchers have identified three vulnerabilities in Wyze Cam v3. They are tracked as CVE-\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6322\"\u003E2023-6322\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6323\"\u003ECVE-2023-6323\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, and \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6324\"\u003ECVE-2023-6324\u003C\u002Fa\u003E. Chained together, these vulnerabilities allow an attacker to obtain root access from the local network. In this case, command execution on the Wyze Cam v3 is obtained via \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6322\"\u003ECVE-2023-6322\u003C\u002Fa\u003E - a stack-based buffer overflow vulnerability in the handler of IOCTL message 0x284C that is used to set the motion detection zone.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA technical dive into the vulnerabilities and how they are daisy-chained to compromise the Wyze Cam v3 is available below:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003C!--kg-card-begin: html--\u003E\u003Cp align=\"center\"\u003E\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Ffiles\u002F2024\u002F05\u002FBitdefender-PReport-Wyze-7745.pdf\" class=\"button-view-all d-inline-block\"\u003EDownload the whitepaper\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003C!--kg-card-end: html--\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"roku-indoor-camera-se\"\u003ERoku Indoor Camera SE\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe vulnerabilities in the Roku Indoor Camera SE are identical to those in Wyze Cam v3 (and potentially other security cameras). Bitdefender researchers have daisy-chained CVE-\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6322\"\u003E2023-6322\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6323\"\u003ECVE-2023-6323\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, and \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2023-6324\"\u003ECVE-2023-6324\u003C\u002Fa\u003Eto obtain the necessary prerequisites for talking to the camera and for running OS commands as root user.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA technical dive into the vulnerabilities and how they are daisy-chained to compromise the Roku Indoor Camera SE is available below:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003C!--kg-card-begin: html--\u003E\u003Cp align=\"center\"\u003E\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Ffiles\u002F2024\u002F05\u002FBitdefender-PReport-roku-7745.pdf\" class=\"button-view-all d-inline-block\"\u003EDownload the whitepaper\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003C!--kg-card-end: html--\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"implications-and-remediation\"\u003EImplications and Remediation:\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe ramifications of these vulnerabilities extend far beyond the realm of theoretical exploits, as they directly impact on the privacy and safety of users relying on devices powered by ThroughTek Kalay. Our findings have been responsibly disclosed both to the platform vendor, as well as to the tested integrators. APAC's malvertising ecosystem: How scams spread through social media ads",slug:"inside-the-apac-malvertising-ecosystem",feature_image:"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F06\u002FAPAC.png",featured:d,published_at:"2026-06-02T02:30:18.000+03:00",custom_excerpt:a,html:"\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender Labs has uncovered a large-scale malvertising ecosystem operating across APAC, where scam campaigns are \u003Cstrong\u003Edistributed through paid advertising on Meta platforms\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E and quickly generate massive reach.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-takeaways\"\u003EKey takeaways\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EBitdefender Labs identified \u003Cstrong\u003E12,000 scam campaigns\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E across 13 APAC countries\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EThese campaigns generated more than \u003Cstrong\u003E400,000 ad sightings\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E through paid ads on Meta platforms\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EHealth and finance are the leading scam categories\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, together accounting for \u003Cstrong\u003E37.3%\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E of all campaigns\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EAustralia alone accounts for 52%\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E of all observed scam campaigns\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EThree repeatable cross-country patterns stand out: fake app downloads, scandal-driven phishing, and AI-themed investment scams\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EAcross markets, scammers reuse infrastructure, fake pages, redirect chains, and campaign templates\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBetween January and April 2026, \u003Cstrong\u003EAlexandra Svetlana DINULICA and Vlad Mihai Sireanu of \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EBitdefender Labs tracked more than \u003Cstrong\u003E400,000 scam ad sightings\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E tied to over \u003Cstrong\u003E12,000 scam campaigns\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E across 13 APAC countries. While these fraudulent ads seem unrelated at first glance, promoting everything from health products to crypto apps and celebrity stories, they follow the same playbook.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMany of the finance-focused campaigns mirror tactics we documented earlier this year in our global investment scam network that abused Meta’s ad system. In both cases, scammers rely on fake news narratives, impersonated brands, celebrity endorsements, and coordinated redirect infrastructure to move users from a trusted-looking ad into a fraudulent environment. The malvertising APAC data shows that these techniques are reused at scale.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"a-malvertising-ecosystem-hiding-in-plain-sight\"\u003EA malvertising ecosystem hiding in plain sight\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne of the most important takeaways is how broad this ecosystem really is. Health-related scams lead the dataset at \u003Cstrong\u003E19%\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, followed by finance at \u003Cstrong\u003E18%\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E. After that, the categories spread out into entertainment, home, gambling, courses, beauty, software, and more.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESocial media ads have effectively become testing grounds for scammers. Some campaigns lean into financial anxiety. Others push health fears, celebrity gossip, or “exclusive” opportunities. The themes shift depending on the audience, but the objective stays the same: get the click before the user has time to question what they’re seeing.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"different-themes-same-machinery\"\u003EDifferent themes, same machinery\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EEven though the ad content varies, the underlying structure rarely does.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA user sees a paid ad that looks legitimate. It may feature a trusted brand, a well-known personality, or a news source that looks real. In some cases, the preview even shows a legitimate domain.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThen comes the redirect.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAfter the click, users are sent through one or more intermediary pages before landing on a fake website, a phishing form, or a malicious download. These destinations can rotate constantly, making the campaigns harder to detect and take down.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe same pattern shows up again and again, across countries and categories.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EThe playbooks behind health-themed malvertising campaigns\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe observed health scam ecosystem spans several major categories, including sleep disorder and anti-snoring device scams, \"whistleblower doctor\" respiratory remedy scams, health insurance \"hack\" scams, and weight loss or metabolism supplement scams.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;The analyzed campaigns are all built around highly sophisticated, deceptive advertising campaigns that exploit consumer fears and health vulnerabilities through emotionally persuasive storytelling, fake expert authority, and pseudo-scientific claims. These campaigns commonly promote unverified health remedies, insurance \"loopholes,\" and wellness products using fabricated testimonials, conspiracy narratives, manipulated medical data, and misleading urgency tactics designed to build trust and drive conversions. Common patterns include impersonation of medical professionals, claims of suppressed or \"hidden\" solutions, suspicious newly created domains, lead-generation funnels, and exaggerated promises targeting vulnerable audiences.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F06\u002FScreenshot-2026-05-20-102949--2-.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"371\" height=\"677\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F06\u002FScreenshot-2026-05-20-102818--1---1---1-.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"377\" height=\"676\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"the-playbooks-behind-the-financial-themed-scam-campaigns\"\u003EThe playbooks behind the financial-themed scam campaigns\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"fake-apps-and-download-traps\"\u003EFake apps and download traps\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne of the most common tactics is to impersonate platforms like Binance, TradingView, or Wise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe ad looks convincing. It might offer a bonus, a premium upgrade, or a desktop app download. The preview looks legitimate, but the destination leads to a fake site designed to steal credentials or install malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis pattern appears in Vietnam, Japan, Bangladesh, Thailand, Malaysia, New Zealand, and the Philippines, often using near-identical infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F06\u002FScreenshot-2026-05-19-110757--1---1-.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"379\" height=\"526\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F06\u002FScreenshot-2026-05-19-105500--1---1-.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"376\" height=\"564\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F06\u002FScreenshot-2026-05-19-110534--1---1-.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"377\" height=\"674\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"scandal-and-celebrity-bait\"\u003EScandal and celebrity bait\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother approach leans heavily on trust.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EScammers create fake “breaking news” stories involving central banks, economists, or celebrities. These ads are designed to feel urgent and credible at the same time, pushing users to click quickly.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe see this in campaigns tied to the Reserve Bank of Australia, Bank Negara Malaysia, and celebrity figures in Japan and Bangladesh. &nbsp;This is the same playbook we saw in the Meta-based investment scam network\u003Cstrong\u003E \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Fglobal-investment-scam-network-using-meta-ads\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003Ereported\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003Eby Bitdefender Labs in March, now reappearing across APAC markets with new branding and new audiences.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fdata-src-image-1da38c3e-b1f0-4d5c-b344-85a72fde222c.png\" scams\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe third pattern is investment fraud. Instead of promising profits directly, these campaigns talk of “AI-powered insights,” “stock diagnostics,” or automated strategies.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"where-scam-campaigns-are-most-active\"\u003EWhere scam campaigns are most active\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002FAPAC-Malvertising.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1536\" height=\"1024\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002FAPAC-Malvertising.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F05\u002FAPAC-Malvertising.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002FAPAC-Malvertising.png 1536w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAustralia clearly dominates, but in the rest of APAC, scam campaigns are widely distributed and constantly shifting.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAcross APAC, scam ads don’t look the same, but their behavior is very similar. In Australia, the scams often feel polished and convincing, sometimes posing as breaking news or using familiar names to build trust. In India, it’s less about storytelling and more about scale, with the same message pushed through dozens of fake accounts at once. In Southeast Asia, you start to see both approaches combined, with fake apps, investment offers, and impersonated brands appearing across multiple countries with only minor tweaks.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn some markets, scammers focus on making things feel local. In Bangladesh, for example, ads use the local language and familiar public figures, while in Singapore, some campaigns go a step further by using real financial data to make fake tools look legitimate. In Indonesia, the strategy shifts again, with low-cost offers used to start conversations that quickly move to private messages rather than websites. These differences make the scams feel tailored, even though the same techniques are being reused behind the scenes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhat really stands out is how closely everything is connected. The same fake apps, the same types of investment scams, and even the same accounts can show up in multiple countries at once. Some campaigns are clearly built to run across borders, while others spill over into new markets as they gain momentum. So even if the ads look different depending on where you are, they’re often part of the same larger system, one that keeps adapting but rarely changes its core playbook.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"how-users-across-apac-can-stay-safe\"\u003EHow users across APAC can stay safe\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe campaigns we analyzed may look different depending on where you are, but they rely on the same core tricks: urgency, trust, and misdirection. Whether it’s a bogus health product, a trading platform, or a breaking news story, the goal is to make you click before you have time to question what you’re seeing.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThat’s why slowing down and double-checking can make a real difference.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIf you come across a “shocking” investment story or a too-good-to-be-true opportunity, you can run it through \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Fscamio\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Scamio\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E. Just paste the link, message, or even a screenshot, and it will quickly flag common scam patterns, including fake investment platforms, impersonation campaigns, and urgent “deposit now” schemes like the ones uncovered in this investigation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIt’s also worth checking where a link actually leads before clicking. Many of the ads we analyzed displayed trusted domains in the preview, only to redirect users to a completely different destination. \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Flink-checker\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Link Checker\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E helps uncover those hidden redirects and flag unsafe websites before you share any personal information.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOn a desktop, having protection running in the background adds another layer of safety. A full Bitdefender security solution for Windows or macOS can block phishing pages, fake news clones, and fraudulent landing pages automatically, even if you click on a malicious ad by mistake.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnd since most of these scams start on mobile, protecting your phone is equally important. With \u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Mobile Security\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E on \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Fmobile-security-android\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EAndroid\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003Eor\u003Cstrong\u003E \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Fmobile-security-ios\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EiOS\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, you’re protected against malicious links, scam-driven redirects, and unsafe websites that often originate from social media apps and sponsored posts. Moreover, with Scam Radar, you get \u003Cstrong\u003Ereal-time alerts about scam campaigns that are actively spreading\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E in your area. It acts as an early warning system, giving you a heads-up before you even encounter the scam.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EEach alert includes real examples of scam messages or ads, along with the tactics, keywords, and links attackers are using. You also get insight into who scammers are impersonating, whether it’s a well-known brand, a public figure, or a financial platform. That context makes it much easier to recognize the same pattern when it shows up in your fans are increasingly targeted by scams exploiting club loyalty, national teams, football collectibles, streaming demand, and the growing excitement around the FIFA World Cup 2026, according to Bitdefender Labs.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOur most recent investigation uncovered more than 55 football-related malvertising campaigns targeting users through fake online stores, social media ads, IPTV piracy operations, fraudulent football apps, and FIFA-themed giveaway and lottery scams distributed through email.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-takeaways\"\u003EKey takeaways\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EResearchers identified more than 55 football-related scam ad campaigns targeting fans on Meta-owned platforms. for malvertising campaigns promoting fake football merchandise and phishing pages.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EScam campaigns targeted supporters of England, Scotland, the Tartan Army, and Hearts FC.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EFake Panini sticker offers and football collectibles were promoted through malicious ads and fake storefronts.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EBitdefender Antispam Lab identified multiple FIFA-themed lottery and giveaway scams delivered through email.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EResearchers uncovered coordinated IPTV piracy operations and fake football apps targeting football fans in Portugal, Algeria, and other regions.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003ESeveral fake merchandise operations were linked to organized overseas operators using a structured advertising infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"football-fans-are-being-targeted-through-social-media-malvertising\"\u003EFootball fans are being targeted through social media malvertising\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAs excitement builds for the FIFA World Cup 2026, cybercriminals have been capitalizing on global football enthusiasm for months through a growing ecosystem of scams, fake shops, phishing campaigns, and fraudulent social media operations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne of the clearest trends observed by researchers Alexandra Dinulica and Vlad Mihai Sireanu during their investigation was the aggressive use of malicious advertising campaigns targeting football supporters on social media platforms, particularly Facebook and Meta services.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers identified scam ads promoting:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E“Official” FIFA World Cup merchandise\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EEngland and Scotland football kits\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EHearts FC-themed clothing\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EPanini sticker packs and albums\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003ELimited-edition football collectibles\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EFootball streaming services\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EFake fan gear and ticket offers\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMany of the ads were designed to blend naturally into users’ feeds using realistic product photos, football imagery, countdown timers, and pressure tactics such as: “Limited stock”, “Today only” &nbsp;or “Selling out fast” .\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fdata-src-image-b606f30e-deb2-4e2c-8606-76c5b22b6d61.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"334\" height=\"673\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fdata-src-image-fff94b2c-da60-4de0-8b71-1df8063b76d8.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"362\" height=\"598\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe ads redirected users to fake online stores and phishing pages designed to collect payment details or personal information.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESeveral suspicious domains repeatedly appeared throughout the investigation, including:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003Efaithoutfit[.]uk\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003Edefwear[.]uk\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003Esavebigwear[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003Eteamcollections[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003Efanzonewear[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003Ecrestwearus[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe identified scam campaigns targeted England supporters using “Three Lions” branding, Scotland national team supporters, Tartan Army fans and Hearts FC supporters.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearcher Ionut Baltariu also observed football-themed scam ads promoting supposed FIFA World Cup 2026 Panini sticker albums and collectibles as early as February 2026. Several fake pages promoting these ads also contained noticeable branding inconsistencies and typos. In one example, a page used the name “WordCup” instead of “World Cup,” while still trying to imitate official football branding and Panini-related promotions. Other campaigns used names such as “Loja Panini,” “Ofcpanini,” and “Brasil Hexa 2026” to appear connected to legitimate football collectibles and World Cup merchandise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe ads heavily targeted Portuguese-speaking audiences, particularly users in Brazil, and promoted alleged pre-orders for “official” FIFA World Cup 2026 sticker albums, bundles, and collector kits. Multiple versions of the same ads were seen running simultaneously across Facebook and Instagram, a common malvertising tactic to maximize reach and test engagement. We also noticed signs that some football-themed scam ads may have relied on AI-generated or heavily AI-enhanced promotional imagery. Several campaigns used unusually polished visuals, synthetic-looking product renders, and inconsistent branding elements to create advertisements for fake FIFA World Cup 2026 sticker albums and collectibles.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fdata-src-image-18a2f394-c61f-4767-9c8c-bf7e35f76639.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" 600w\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg id=\"chinese-operated-counterfeit-football-merchandise-campaigns\"\u003EChinese-operated counterfeit football merchandise campaigns\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also identified multiple counterfeit football merchandise scam operations linked to organized overseas operators.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETwo fake merchandise campaigns targeting UK football supporters were definitively attributed to Chinese operators after investigators discovered Simplified Chinese UTM campaign parameters embedded directly inside advertising tracking infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBecause these labels appeared directly inside advertising tracking parameters, they provide unusually strong attribution evidence linking the operations to Chinese-speaking operators running structured malvertising campaigns.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe wording also suggests organized advertising workflows involving campaign testing, bid optimization, and multi-storefront management rather than isolated scam activity.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also observed identical ad copy and similar storefront structures across multiple domains, indicating operators were running several fake shops simultaneously so they could continue operating if one website was removed or blocked.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"football-kit-scam-campaigns-targeting-parents\"\u003EFootball kit scam campaigns targeting parents \u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAmong the findings, one operation stood out due to its clear focus on parents shopping for children’s football kits.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fdata-src-image-647ed6ba-d665-4ac4-b891-63b53f8f76d7.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"354\" height=\"603\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe scam campaign, tied to the website malskitukpatch.com and operating under the name “PrimeFinds UK,” advertised football kits for children between 3 and 13 years old.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe website tried to look trustworthy by claiming:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E“Dispatched from UK in 24hrs”\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E“Free UK shipping”\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E“30-day returns”\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers found no evidence to support these claims. The products were likely shipped from overseas suppliers, meaning buyers may face long delivery times, poor-quality products, or difficulty obtaining refunds.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe branding itself appears designed to create a sense of legitimacy. The name “PrimeFinds UK” suggests a local UK retailer and resembles naming conventions commonly associated with trusted e-commerce platforms.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also found that the same operator promoted unrelated products through other websites, including dog car seat covers. This pattern is commonly associated with low-trust scam or dropshipping operations that quickly pivot between trending products and seasonal events.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"fifa-lottery-and-giveaway-scams-targeting-fans\"\u003EFIFA lottery and giveaway scams targeting fans\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender Antispam Lab researcher Viorel Zavoiu identified multiple email scam campaigns impersonating FIFA World Cup 2026 organizations and promotional programs.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe emails falsely claimed recipients had won cash prizes through FIFA lotteries, online giveaways, or promotional draws connected to upcoming football events.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg 624w\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESome messages promised winnings of up to $2 million and instructed victims to contact so-called “claims agents” or verification offices to process their reward.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe scams impersonated entities such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EFIFA Legal and Compliance Division\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EFIFA World Cup 2026 Local Organizing Committee\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EFIFA Awards promotional programs\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo appear legitimate, the emails included reference numbers, Ticket IDs, Office addresses, legal terminology and even “confidential” PIN codes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne version instructed recipients to contact a purported claims agent in South Africa using a free Gmail address, and to request personal information such as nationality, occupation, address, and phone number.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother variation requested passport or national identification details early in the process, suggesting potential risks of identity theft in addition to financial fraud.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese campaigns follow the structure of classic advance-fee and lottery scams, but their use of FIFA branding and World Cup references makes them more believable to football fans who are expecting promotions, giveaways, ticket draws, and tournament-related offers.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers observed these scams operating alongside broader football-themed fraud, including fake merchandise stores, ticket scams, IPTV piracy services, counterfeit collectibles, and malvertising campaigns spread through social media.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"illegal-iptv-and-fake-football-apps\"\u003EIllegal IPTV and Fake Football Apps\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe investigation also uncovered football-themed piracy and fake app scam campaigns targeting fans seeking match streams and football content.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn Portugal, researchers linked UniTV and Xtream IPTV through shared entities and overlapping infrastructure, suggesting a single operator managing multiple IPTV brands simultaneously.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis type of setup allows operators to continue functioning even if one service is disrupted or removed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also identified coordinated fake football app scam campaigns connected to “Goal Rush” and “BEST APP,” which deployed dozens of fake entities across multiple operations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESome used Cyrillic character spoofing to evade moderation systems and automated detection tools, suggesting a more advanced and coordinated operation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"how-to-stay-safe\"\u003EHow to Stay Safe\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA few basic habits can significantly reduce the risk of falling victim to football\u002F FIFA World Cup &nbsp;scams:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EBe cautious with football-related ads and promotions appearing on social media, especially those offering unrealistic discounts, fake giveaways, or “limited-time” deals.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EAvoid clicking unsolicited links related to FIFA World Cup tickets, football merchandise, streaming platforms, or Panini collectibles.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EType official website addresses manually whenever possible instead of relying on links inside ads or emails.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003ETreat countdown timers and aggressive urgency messages as warning signs rather than reasons to rush.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EUse scam detection tools such as \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Fscamio\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Scamio\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E and \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Flink-checker\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Link Checker\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E to analyze suspicious messages, screenshots, websites, and redirects before interacting with them.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EUse a security solution on your desktop and mobile devices to help block phishing pages, malicious redirects, fake storefronts, and scam websites distributed through social media ads and messaging apps.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fblog\u002Fhotforsecurity\u002Fscam-radar-bitdefender\" href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Ffamily\" rel=\"noreferrer\"\u003Eall-in-one security suites\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, provides early warnings about active scam campaigns before you become a target. It uses AI and advanced threat intelligence to identify emerging scam trends, including fake shopping ads, phishing attempts, and social engineering campaigns, while explaining how they work and how to avoid them.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIf you believe you may have already interacted with a scam:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EContact your bank or card provider immediately if payment details were submitted\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMonitor your accounts closely for suspicious activity or follow-up phishing attempts\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003ETreat passport, ID, or personal information shared with suspicious websites as potentially compromised\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EKeep screenshots, receipts, emails, and website addresses if purchased goods never arrive\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EReport the scam to local fraud reporting or consumer protection authorities\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMost importantly, verify offers through official channels whenever possible. That means using FIFA.com for tournament information and tickets, recognized retailers for football merchandise and collectibles, and official streaming providers for live matches.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EDisclaimer: \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EThis report is published for informational and educational purposes only. Labs researchers have been tracking smishing campaigns targeting drivers on a global scale. The scam campaigns are still active as of April 2026\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EOver 79,000 fraudulent messages\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E have already been detected in 40 distinct SMS scam campaigns\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EThe scams impersonate \u003Cstrong\u003EDMVs, toll operators, and parking authorities\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E from all over the world\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EVictims are redirected to \u003Cstrong\u003Efake payment sites or, in some cases, malware downloads\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EIts infrastructure is characterized by rapid domain generation, sender-ID spoofing, and multiple evasion techniques targeting mobile operating systems\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESince \u003Cstrong\u003EDecember 2025 through April 2026\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, Bitdefender Labs has been monitoring ongoing smishing campaigns targeting drivers worldwide.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESo far, according to Labs’ researcher Alecsandru Catalin Daj, fraudsters used:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E79,000+ scam text messages (over 29,200 unique variants)\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EOur anti-phishing systems \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003Edetected over \u003Cstrong\u003E31,900 different URLs spreading through these unsolicited text messages\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EActivity across \u003Cstrong\u003Ea dozen countries\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, including the US, Canada, Australia, New Zealand, France, Spain, Colombia, Brazil, India, the UK, Ireland, and Luxembourg\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMessages delivered in multiple languages, including \u003Cstrong\u003EEnglish, Spanish, Portuguese, French, and Hindi\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAll messages share a common goal: to persuade recipients to pay a fake fine, hand over sensitive information or install spyware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhile the activity is widespread and highly coordinated, \u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Labs has not attributed these scams to a specific threat actor or group\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt this stage, there’s no confirmed link tying these campaigns together, beyond a shared theme of \u003Cstrong\u003Emessages about unpaid tolls, parking violations, or traffic fines.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhat connects them are similarities in tactics, messaging, and delivery methods, rather than a clearly identified single source.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch1 id=\"how-the-scams-work\"\u003EHow the scams work\u003C\u002Fh1\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese scam messages are designed to create a sense of urgency and pressure drivers into acting quickly. A typical message claims that the recipient has an unpaid toll, traffic fine, or parking ticket and sets a short deadline, usually between 24 and 72 hours, to resolve the issue. To increase the sense of risk, the message often threatens consequences such as additional fees, license suspension, legal action, or even arrest warrants. It then prompts the recipient to click a link to “resolve” the matter.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThat link leads to a fraudulent website designed to closely resemble an official government or toll payment portal. Once there, victims may be asked to enter sensitive information, including card details, personal data, and in some cases, banking credentials. In certain regions, the attack goes a step further by delivering malware instead of a payment page.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg
